Mrs Chain took the paper from Beatrix. "Identity trimming," she repeated. Lolly nodded, once. Mrs Chain looked back at her. "That means me," she managed, with a terrible steadiness.\
"Yes."

Lolly's hands, she noticed, had gone entirely still, which was not the same thing as calm.

The house, perhaps sensing that it had been discussed too accurately, made a small sound overhead. Not a creak. More a correction.

Then, from the sitting room, came the flat electronic ding of an arriving message.

They returned to find the rectangular mirror in the hall faintly lit. Words were appearing across its surface in blue.

> WELCOME, RESIDENT
>
> YOUR DOMESTIC EXPERIENCE HAS BEEN UPDATED FOR GREATER CONSISTENCY
>
> PLEASE REPORT ANY REMAINING ATTACHMENT TO PREVIOUS LAYOUTS

Mrs Chain set down the paper and picked up the umbrella from the stand.

Lolly, recognising the posture of a woman about to improve technology by force, warned, "Possibly not---"

Too late. She struck the mirror smartly across the middle. The text vanished. The mirror cracked, and for the first time all day something in the house seemed honestly itself.

Jago let out a breath that might almost have been admiration. "That," he said, "felt clinically significant."

Beatrix bent to inspect the broken frame. Behind it, fixed to the plaster, was a small grey module no larger than a cigarette case, humming very softly.

Lolly stared. "What is that."

Beatrix prised it loose. On the back was stamped:

> CIVIC STABILITY SUBSTRATE: HOME EDITION

Beneath it, in smaller type:

Property of the Simplification Initiative

Lolly took the device in both hands. It was warm. Too warm. "It's local," she breathed. "They're installing reality correction hardware in people's houses."

Mrs Chain looked at the module for a long moment: the particular look, Lolly thought, of a woman doing sums that would not come out in her favour whichever way she added them. "I don't know what happens if you take that out," she decided at last. "Nobody's told me, and nobody's going to. Remove it anyway."

Lolly turned, uncertain, to Beatrix. "Do it," Beatrix instructed.

Jago stepped back into the doorway. "Before you do, a practical note. If this thing is part of a wider correction network, unplugging it may tell someone that Unit 7B has become ideologically restless."\
"Excellent," Mrs Chain replied, tightening her grip on the umbrella.

Lolly turned the device over. There was a recessed catch on one edge, and the old habit rose in her: write it down first, decide later, let the notebook carry the risk. She did not reach for the notebook. She pressed the catch. The module clicked.

For one instant nothing happened. Then everything in the room became uncertain.

The clock vanished, reappeared, and turned into a photograph of a man with spectacles. The daffodils browned into geraniums, then both at once. The carpet shifted from stripes to swirls to bare boards and back.

Somewhere upstairs, a door opened and shut in several incompatible styles.

Mrs Chain grasped the banister.

Lolly felt the air thicken around her, not physically but informationally, as though the house had ceased to know which version of itself it had been ordered to become.

She opened the notebook with one hand and wrote almost blindly:

System disturbed. Underlying states still present, not yet [collapsed](#glCollapse) to anything in particular. [The false certainty had only been holding them down](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wXJ9eQ7qTQk).

Beside her Beatrix hissed, "Wren."

She looked up. At the far end of the hall, where the front door should have shown a small rectangle of afternoon light, there was now a man in a dark coat standing just inside the threshold. No one had heard him enter.

He held a slim case and wore the polished smile of someone whose job consisted of describing intolerable things as procedural necessities.

He inclined his head. "Good afternoon," he intoned. "Continuity Review."

Mrs Chain lifted the umbrella a little higher. The man's smile remained in place with professional courage. "I'm afraid," he continued, "someone appears to have interrupted an authorised domestic convergence." He glanced at a slim watch that seemed, on closer inspection, to be showing more than one time at once, and did not appear troubled by it. "We can still complete the visit today," he added, "if everyone remains reasonable."

Lolly looked down at the humming module, at the cracked mirror, at the photograph still trying to become a clock, and then back at the man in the doorway. "Oh," she said. "Was that yours."

At the end of the corridor, a door that had been there ten minutes earlier was no longer there. Lolly wrote its number down before she could forget it. When she checked again, the number was still in the notebook. The door was not.

That was the first time she understood that the Ministry was no longer merely recording errors in reality. It was beginning to acquire them.

Rule: A measurement gets its answer by touching the system, which is why asking too soon can spoil what the system might otherwise have shown.

Lesson for this chapter: [Premature Collapse](#lesson-3-premature-collapse)

# **Chapter 4: Continuity Review**

The man in Mrs Chain's doorway was not damp, which only served to make the rest of him more offensive. He was in a dark coat of almost expensive sobriety, with a narrow tie and a face that looked as though it had once tried charm and settled, after a risk assessment, on procedure.

In his right hand he carried a slim case of brushed grey metal. In his left he held a card badge displaying a silver spiral and the words:

> MINISTRY OF CIVIC STABILITY
>
> CONTINUITY REVIEW

Field Resolution

Mrs Elspeth Chain tightened her grip on the umbrella.

Lolly Wren looked down at the warm grey module in her hands, then back at the visitor. "Yes," she replied. "That does seem, in hindsight, to have occurred."

The man stepped one shoe inside the threshold, then paused as if waiting for the house to endorse him. It did not, exactly, but a lamp in the sitting room brightened by a degree that felt sycophantic.

"My name is Alistair Venn," he announced. "Senior Review Officer, Continuity. If you'd all remain calm, this can be resolved locally."

Mrs Chain gave a short laugh of such pure disbelief that even the daffodils in the hall seemed to reconsider themselves. "Mr Venn," she informed him, "you have entered my house during a metaphysical burglary and used the word locally."

"I do understand this is a distressing transition."\
"It is not a transition," Mrs Chain corrected. "It is vandalism with signage."

Beatrix Sloan had already moved to one side of the hall, arms folded, wearing the look of an auditor preparing to improve a man's afternoon beyond recovery. "Show me your authority," she demanded.

Venn smiled. "Internal Audit."\
"Yes."\
"How comforting."\
"No," Beatrix clarified, "specific."

Venn set the slim case on the hall table (teal, Lolly noticed with renewed disgust) and removed a creased document stamped with too many approvals to be morally sound.

"Phase One continuity authority," he recited. "Regional domestic stabilisation. Sub-clause twelve permits local correction where unresolved identity adhesion threatens public confidence. Fail to resolve locally, and the file moves to Phase Two review, at which point I am no longer the worst thing to come through your door."

He said it lightly, the way a man says a threat he has grown tired of dressing up as a warning.

Mrs Chain pointed the umbrella at him. "Public confidence in what."\
Venn's smile remained in place with admirable institutional training. "In dwellings, among other things."

Jago Flint, who had taken up a position against the wall in the vague manner of a man unwilling to commit to being fully in one room, said, "That's not an answer, is it."

"It is the one currently issued," Venn replied.

Lolly, still holding the detached module, felt the house shifting minutely around her. The cracked mirror in the hall was attempting to decide whether it had always been cracked, and the clock in the sitting room had, for the moment, become a photograph again. One sensed a large number of small possibilities pressing upward through a badly fitted administrative lid.

Venn noticed the module in Lolly's hand. "If you'd return that to me," he suggested, "I can re-establish coherence."

Her hand twitched towards him before she told it not to. It was an old habit: a man in a tie asks, you hand it over, you apologise for the inconvenience of having held it at all. She kept her fingers closed. "What exactly does it do."

"It supports stable domestic identity."\
"By replacing people's teacups," countered Mrs Chain, "and misfiling their dead."\
Venn gave her a patient look. "By reducing low-value divergence."

There are phrases which, once spoken aloud, remove any residual doubt as to whether a person deserves tea.

Beatrix snapped, "Define low-value."\
"Features whose emotional significance exceeds their civic necessity."\
Mrs Chain stared at him. "You ghastly man."

Venn ignored this with professional ease. "Now then. If we proceed promptly, most residual variance can be contained. I would rather not still be here when the branch count climbs any further."

He opened the slim case. Inside lay a small handheld scanner, three blank forms, a silver pen, and a set of coloured tabs arranged with a precision that suggested neatness had, for him, stopped being a habit and become a creed.

The pen, Lolly noticed, was already uncapped, as if he had expected to need it before he'd so much as knocked.

A small, cold audit opened somewhere under Lolly's ribs. "What do the tabs do," she asked.\
"Nothing dramatic," Venn explained. "They classify continuity pressure."\
"That is a dramatic sentence," Jago noted.

Venn selected a pale yellow tab and affixed it to the top of one form. "Site unstable," he murmured. "Occupant resistant. Decorative conflict moderate."

Mrs Chain made a sound like a kettle about to begin legal proceedings.

Then Venn raised the scanner and pointed it at the mantelpiece. A soft tone sounded, official and almost gentle. The photograph of the spectacled man flickered and became, for half a second, the clock again: a small, forced [collapse](#glCollapse), over before anyone could object.

Lolly stepped forward. "Stop that."\
Venn lowered the device. "Mr---" "Wren."

"Ms Wren, your interference is understandable, but not helpful. The property is currently exhibiting unresolved domestic supernumeraries. Until I determine which elements belong to the authorised continuity path, the environment may display minor symbolic agitation."

"Symbolic agitation," said Beatrix. "Is that what you call deleting a woman's life."\
"No," remarked Venn. "That would be an escalation."

Silence settled over the room. Even Jago seemed briefly at a loss for an appropriate angle of insolence.

Lolly had seen a single collapse happen once before. Nobody had mentioned, back then, what happened if you did it twice. Three times. As many times as a form required.

She looked at the scanner rather than at him. "You're testing the house object by object."\
"Of course."\
"And each time you test something, how many versions are left afterward?"\
Venn tilted his head. "That is a somewhat theatrical way of putting it."\
"It's a question."\
"One," he admitted, before he had quite decided to answer it. "One version is left."\
"Every time."\
"Every time."

Lolly almost asked how many versions a house was entitled to before breakfast. She suspected the answer would come laminated, and that it would be one.

"Then you're not finding the true version of this house," Lolly pointed out. "You're the reason there's only one left to find."\
Venn recovered himself with visible effort. "Accuracy," he replied, "is not always the same thing as usefulness."

This was, Lolly felt, a remark that ought to disqualify a man from discussing reality.

Venn turned to Mrs Chain. "I'll need to confirm a few details to reduce remaining branch pressure."\
"No," Mrs Chain stated.\
"I'm afraid yes."\
"No," she repeated, with the sort of clarity one associates with church bells and artillery.

Venn consulted the form as if the form might lend him courage. "Was your late husband," he asked, "habitually visible in the front room."

Mrs Chain blinked. "What."\
"On display," Venn clarified. "Photographic representation, commemorative object, or emotional proxy."

Lolly shut her eyes briefly.

Beatrix objected, "You cannot possibly think that is an admissible question."

Venn sighed the sigh of a man burdened by other people's attachment to language. "We have found that unresolved bereavement often creates object density around obsolete personal states."

Mrs Chain advanced one step. "My husband," she informed him, "was not an obsolete personal state."

She said it knowing exactly how it would go on the form. She said it anyway.

Venn made a small note. "High memorial adhesion," he murmured.\
Jago straightened from the wall. "I wouldn't write that down if I were you."

Venn, not yet appreciating the counsel, continued. "Now then. Sister present or absent in core domestic narrative?"

Mrs Chain simply glanced at him.

Venn tried again. "Would you say your sister is structurally central to your self-model."

Mrs Chain turned to Lolly. "Ms Wren."\
"Yes."\
"If I strike him, will the paperwork improve."\
"No," Lolly replied, honestly. "But I am beginning to see its appeal."

Venn raised the scanner again, this time towards the bookshelves. The titles shimmered. Pleasant Soups Through the Year became Regional Stews for Quiet Evenings, then Domestic Warmth: A Practical Guide, then settled once more on soup.

Lolly stared. The scanner was not detecting a stable reality. It was forcing a choice each time it asked. Question. Narrowing. Selection. She knew the name for it, from a textbook she had never expected to need in a hallway: watch a system closely enough, and often enough, and the watching itself can hold it exactly where it started: [the Quantum Zeno Effect](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dOY0NbR1ppw). Nobody who wrote the textbooks had thought to mention what became of the versions that lost.

She opened the green notebook and wrote quickly:

Repeated inspection. Narrower answers. The form decides what survives.

Underneath that she added:

The house is larger than the form.

There was a drier word for what each individual scan was doing, one her books used without apology: state reduction. Snap the whole spread of what a thing could be down to the one branch narrow enough to fit on a form. Do that often enough, faster than the spread can widen back out, and you get exactly the freezing she had just named.

She glanced up and pointed out, "You're not restoring anything. You're compressing it."

Venn glanced over with mild interest, as one might regard a spaniel that had unexpectedly solved an invoice. "Compression," he remarked, "is an inelegant term."\
"It's exact."\
"It is practical."\
"It's theft." This came from Mrs Chain, with such force that even Venn chose not to answer at once.

Then, from upstairs, came a crash. Everyone looked up.

Venn frowned. "That shouldn't happen."\
"Good," Jago said. "I enjoy it when systems lose confidence."

Another crash followed, then the sound of a drawer opening in what Lolly would have sworn were at least two contradictory bedrooms.

Venn stepped towards the stairs. "Please remain here."

Beatrix moved at once to block him. "No."\
"Ms Sloan, this is authorised territory."\
"You've already revised the kitchen into herbal defeat. You don't get the upper floor without witnesses."

Venn considered objecting, thought better of it, and climbed.

They followed in a compact and deeply unwilling procession: Venn first, Beatrix at his shoulder, Lolly with the notebook and the warm module, Mrs Chain gripping the umbrella like the last practical principle in the cosmos, and Jago behind them, carrying no visible authority at all and somehow appearing the least concerned.

Nobody spoke on the stairs. It seemed important, somehow, not to give the house any more ideas.

Upstairs, the landing had changed again. The stickers remained on the doors, but they no longer agreed with the rooms.

BEDROOM A hung over what was plainly a bathroom.

SANITATION UNIT had attached itself to the airing cupboard.

OPTIONAL LINEN SPACE now marked the stairs to the loft.

"That," said Mrs Chain, "is deranged."\
Venn scanned the corridor, and the labels jumped once more, nervously, as though caught gossiping. "Substrate drift," he muttered.

He opened the first door. Inside, what had been earlier a bedroom now existed in a state of elaborate compromise.

Half the wallpaper showed faded blue flowers; the other half had become a tasteful cream stripe. On the bed lay two duvets, one floral, one aggressively neutral, occupying the same territory in open [superposition](#glSuperposition), each with visible resentment.

A framed photograph on the chest of drawers showed Mrs Chain beside the spectacled man from downstairs; another, half-phased into it, showed her alone on a cruise ship she had almost certainly never taken.

Lolly felt the room's arithmetic before she understood it: not errors, but alternatives, still trying to survive: every path the room might have taken, still faintly added in, the way light was said to try every route at once until something made it stop.

She whispered, almost to herself, "They're all still here."\
Venn turned. "What."\
"The discarded versions. The house hasn't lost them. It's suppressing them."\
Beatrix's eyes went sharply from the room to Lolly. "Meaning."

"Meaning every scan buys the authorised version another few minutes of pretending to be the only one."

Jago nodded, as if this were perfectly ordinary conversation to have beside a bed with two duvets. "So the house is pretending harder than it can manage."

Venn's expression tightened for the first time. "Ms Wren, you are not qualified to assess continuity architecture."

"No," Lolly returned. "But I am qualified to notice when a lie requires electricity."\
Venn took a step forward. "Return the substrate module."

Lolly did not. There followed one of those brief, lucid silences in which every person present understands that the day has crossed from paperwork into decision.

Then Jago ventured, "I may be wrong, but is this little grey thing wireless."\
Venn's gaze flicked towards him. "Restricted information."\
"Excellent," Jago declared. "That means yes."

Three years of courier work had taught him more about Ministry hardware than any induction course ever could: mostly which wires people didn't want found.

He held equally firm opinions about locks, though that was a story that generally waited for closing time and a second drink.

He was moving almost before the sentence had ended. One moment he was in the doorway; the next he had crossed the room, lifted the floral duvet, and yanked from beneath the bed a thin cable leading into the skirting board.

"Jago," said Lolly.\
"Trust me."\
"Why would I."\
"No time."

He pulled. The cable came free with a crack and a shower of plaster.

The effect on the house was immediate. Not catastrophic. Worse. Intimate.

The cream stripes on the wall dissolved into blue flowers. The photograph of the cruise ship vanished and was replaced by one of Mrs Chain and a dark-haired woman laughing over some unseen mishap. The neutral duvet slid to the floor.

From downstairs came a rapid sequence of sounds: the kitchen mugs shattering, the herbal sachets dropping like failed policy, a clock giving up halfway through a chime.

Mrs Chain gripped the bedpost. "My sister," she managed, softly.

The woman in the restored photograph was, unmistakably, someone she knew.

Venn swore, and the effect was so startling in a man like him that it briefly improved him.

He lunged towards Lolly for the substrate module. Beatrix intercepted him with the clean, efficient brutality of a person who had spent years carrying boxes of evidence and had made peace with leverage.

Venn hit the wardrobe, rebounded, and sat down abruptly on the edge of the bed with all the dignity of a policy meeting its evidence.

For one unguarded moment, still sitting where he had landed, Venn looked at the restored photograph on the chest of drawers --- the sister, laughing, found again --- with an expression that had nothing to do with continuity architecture at all. Then he remembered himself, and it was gone.

Jago looked genuinely impressed. "Oh, splendid."\
Beatrix did not look at him. "Wren."

Lolly, heart thudding in a most administratively inconvenient way, turned the grey module over in her hands.

On its underside, beneath the Ministry stamp, something had been scratched in tiny letters as if by a previous handler unwilling to trust official channels.

If found active in residential field, contact S. Fainrose.

Lolly read it twice. Then a third time.

"Beatrix," she called.\
"What."

She held out the module.

Beatrix read the inscription, and for the first time since Lolly had known her, her expression shifted from contemptuous control to something much closer to recognition.

"Bloody hell," she managed, quietly.\
Mrs Chain looked up. "What is it."\
Beatrix straightened. "A name."\
"Whose."\
She glanced at Lolly. "Someone who may know how bad this actually is."

Venn, still recovering both breath and authority, protested, "You cannot involve external theoretical parties. Do that, and this stops being a Phase One matter whether I file it or not."

"No," replied Beatrix, tucking the module into her coat. "But we may."

Downstairs, the house continued rearranging itself in unhappy little surges, as if relieved to be less wrong but not yet sure how to be right.

Lolly looked once more at the half-restored room: at the wallpaper finding its conviction, at the single duvet, at the photograph of the sister returned from administrative extinction.

Then she opened the notebook and wrote:

Too much observation can make the wrong world stick.

And below it:

Need Fainrose.

She did not wait to see whether the sentence agreed with her. She was already reaching for her coat.

"Bring the module," instructed Beatrix, without turning. "If we are going to be wrong, we will be wrong with evidence."

They had got as far as the hall, and the hall as far as forgiving them for it, when a car door closed outside with a firmness that suggested the man behind it believed doors ought to be closed firmly, and a knock followed that was less a knock than a statement of intent.

The man on the step was compact, tweed-jacketed, and carried an umbrella he plainly considered a professional instrument rather than a concession to weather. He held up a warrant card with the unhurried satisfaction of someone who had waited his whole career for a doorway worth standing in.

He smelled of wet wool and boot polish, the smell of a man permanently between a rained-on beat and a report not yet written.

Requisitioning a car from the pool meant Form 137 in triplicate and a superintendent's signature that arrived, on a good week, in four working days --- and Priddy's own department measured a good week by how few such forms reached his desk, not by how quickly they left it again.

"Detective Inspector Priddy," he announced. "CID. A neighbour reports criminal damage at this address. Raised voices. A crash. An unfamiliar man was observed letting himself in without, and I quote, 'anyone hearing him enter.' I should like to establish precisely what has occurred here, and in what order."

His voice was flat and precise, built for dictation, not conversation, as though every sentence to come was already halfway to being a statement.

Mrs Chain looked, magnificently, as though she had been rehearsing this moment her entire adult life. "Inspector. Someone has smashed my hall mirror, ruined my wallpaper, and revised my late husband. I would be delighted to give a statement."

Priddy produced a notebook of his own, black, elastic-banded, and opened it to a fresh page with the tenderness of a man greeting an old friend. "Let us begin with the mirror."

It went poorly from there, in the specific way that things go poorly when three witnesses each describe a different crime. Mrs Chain maintained she had struck the mirror herself, deliberately, and would do so again. Jago, when pressed, allowed that he might have "dislodged some cabling," a phrase Priddy wrote down and then underlined twice, unconvinced. Beatrix declined to be interviewed at all, on the stated grounds that she was "not from round here," which was, strictly, true.

He took Lolly in quickly, the way he'd already taken in everything else in the room: middling height, dark hair escaping its clip, and a pencil, never a pen, worn down to a stub behind one ear. Its graphite had left a grey smudge across the pad of her thumb, like a fingerprint she hadn't tried to hide. She looked, he decided, like a woman who had spent a decade fixing things with her hands before someone put her behind a desk instead: a coat that had seen more weather than fashion, no polish to speak of, and the specific stillness of someone who had learned to look unbothered for a living. He filed her, provisionally, as the only witness in the room who might actually be watching him back.

Priddy turned to Lolly last, in the manner of a man saving what he suspected would be the least reliable witness for a position of maximum leverage. "And you, miss?"

"Lolly Wren. I was upstairs. Then downstairs. Possibly both, depending which version of the stairs you mean."\
Priddy's pencil hovered. "I shall want a single, consistent account, Ms Wren."\
"I'd love to give you one," assured Lolly, with perfect sincerity. "The house hasn't been offering many."

He wrote this down, read it back to himself, and underlined the word consistent hard enough that the nib nearly went through the page.

Something in the underlining looked less like disbelief than recognition --- that of a man whose living was reconciling contradictory statements, and who had never once been thanked for it.

"And the gentleman on the floor?" Priddy nodded towards the sitting room, where Venn was attempting to sit up with the injured dignity of a man rebounding from both a wardrobe and a career.

"Gas board," replied Jago, without hesitation. "Contractor. Terrible with pipes."

Priddy noted this, considered Venn's narrow tie and brushed-grey case, and noted something else beneath it that he did not read aloud.

He asked Mrs Chain where she had been at the time of the crash. Upstairs, she said, then downstairs, then both, before settling crossly on downstairs and daring him to disagree. He asked whether her sister lived at the property. Mrs Chain glanced at the mantelpiece, where the photograph had not entirely finished deciding, and said her sister was not currently in residence, which was, at that exact moment, only sort of a lie. He asked about the wallpaper. "It's always been cream," stated Mrs Chain, at the precise instant the last blue flower gave up and vanished, and had the grace not to look at it.

Priddy closed his notebook, opened it again to check something, and closed it a second time with the air of a man declining to trust a conclusion he had not yet been permitted to reach.

"I shall need to establish everyone's whereabouts more formally," he noted. "Every alibi has a hole in it, madam, if one looks closely enough."

"You have no idea," replied Mrs Chain, with feeling.

He left them his card, and the house, in that order, and drove off already composing, one sensed, an extremely thorough report that would satisfy absolutely no one, least of all himself.

The mirror fragments, bagged as evidence, would spend the next several months in a property store two constables were still trying to reconcile against a ledger nobody had audited since the Home Secretary before last, and would eventually be released back to Mrs Chain having proved nothing whatsoever --- which was, in Priddy's experience, roughly his department's success rate.

Rule: Inspect a quantum state often enough in the right physical way, and the inspection itself can help keep it where it was.

Lesson for this chapter: [Repeated Measurement and the Quantum Zeno Effect](#lesson-4-the-quantum-zeno-effect)

# **Chapter 5: Dr Fainrose Is Not Reassured**

Forty minutes after the interruption of Mrs Elspeth Chain's domestic convergence, Beatrix Sloan was in an unlicensed grey car with Lolly Wren, Jago Flint, a confiscated module, Mrs Chain's umbrella, and a woman whose house was still fluctuating. This was witness selection: the third response to institutional catastrophe she considered proper, after documentation and containment.

The car belonged to Jago. Or rather, it belonged to a man in Croydon who believed it was in long-term bonded storage, which was not identical but had, Jago maintained, all the practical advantages of ownership without the correspondence.

The man in Croydon was owed, by Lolly's best reckoning, at least three favours and one long-overdue apology, a ratio Jago considered healthy for a working relationship. He was, by his own count, similarly owed by roughly a third of the Ministry's internal directory, though he had never once tried to collect, on the grounds that a debt called in stopped being useful.

Lolly sat in the back holding the grey module in both hands, as if it might otherwise begin revising her. Mrs Chain sat beside her with the umbrella across her knees. Beatrix occupied the front in concentrated disapproval. Jago drove with one hand and the fatalism of a man who trusted neither maps nor causality.

Once, somewhere past the second roundabout, it warmed faintly against her palms, then went cold again before she could decide whether that was worth mentioning. She decided it wasn't.

When Beatrix finally spoke it was in the same low, even voice she used for threats and directions alike --- precise enough that people found themselves complying before they'd decided to.

"Where exactly," ventured Lolly, for the third time, "are we going."\
Beatrix did not turn round. "Fainrose."\
"That is not a place."\
"No," Jago put in. "It's worse. It's a person."\
Mrs Chain glanced at the module. "The name on the device."\
"Yes," replied Beatrix.

Lolly glanced down at the scratched inscription on the module. If found active in residential field, contact S. Fainrose. She was right. There is something uniquely disheartening about discovering that a person one has not yet met has already won an argument with the universe.

"Who is she," asked Mrs Chain.

Beatrix was silent for a moment, which in her case meant the answer required enough accuracy to be annoying. "Former Ministry physicist," she offered at last. "Systems architecture. Interpretive dynamics. Early transition modelling."

"I'm sorry," Lolly asked. "Are those real subjects."\
"They were funded as if they could be wrought to sit still in a briefing paper."

Jago turned left through a yellow light with the lightness of conscience that comes from never recognising traffic law as binding philosophy. "She used to be very fashionable," he recalled. "Brilliant, impossible, terrifying in committee. Left after some unpleasantness involving continuity theory and a minister who thought eigenstates were a minority voting bloc."

Mrs Chain considered this. "And we trust her."\
"No," Beatrix replied.\
"Good."\
"We require her."\
"Better."

They drove west. The city thinned gradually. Office blocks gave way to terraces, then detached houses, then stretches of overmanaged green. The morning had become one of those bright English afternoons determined to deny the possibility of disaster.

Lolly watched the route display on the dashboard. It altered twice. The first time, their destination changed from Wetherby Lane to Wetherby Approach. The second time it became Fainrose Cottage / Pending Local Address Reconciliation. She sighed. Jago noticed. "You've got to stop taking road systems personally."\
"They've started first."\
"Fair."

At length they turned off the main road into a narrow lane. A wrought-iron gate appeared where Lolly would have sworn there had been no gate. It bore a sign:

> THE INSTITUTE FOR UNHELPFUL CLARITY

Visitors by prior argument only

No refunds for conclusions

A smaller card, tucked behind the first as if ashamed of its own usefulness, read: IF YOU ALREADY KNOW WHAT YOU THINK, THE GATE WILL LOOK LOCKED. Lolly tried the latch. It was unlocked. She disliked this as evidence.

Jago braked.

Mrs Chain leaned forward. "I like her already."

Beyond the gate stood a long, low brick house with a slate roof and the defensive air of a property that had spent years discouraging governments. An old dish antenna had been bolted to one chimney. On the lawn stood a brass armillary sphere and what looked like a weather vane but was almost certainly measuring something less forgivable. By the front step, a boot-scraper had been fitted, for reasons nobody was prepared to ask about, with a small brass plate reading CALIBRATED.

Somewhere behind the brickwork it produced a faint, continuous electronic whine, pitched just high enough to make visitors feel quietly reprimanded without knowing why.

It was turning, slowly and steadily, though the morning itself had gone still --- as if it answered to a wind none of them could feel.

Lolly got out and stared. "This," she guessed, "is where the Ministry's former continuity architect lives."\
"No," remarked Beatrix. "This is where she waits for it to fail."

Before anyone could knock, the front door opened. Stephanie Fainrose was not tall, and not young, and had a stillness that forced other people to feel over-articulated. She wore a dark cardigan, narrow trousers, and spectacles that looked like a final administrative judgement upon the human face.

Her eyes went first to Beatrix, then to Lolly, then to the module in Lolly's hands, and then to Mrs Chain's umbrella.

Her gaze rested on Beatrix a half-second longer than the introduction strictly required. "Sloan."\
"Fainrose." Neither offered anything further, in the manner of two people who had long ago agreed that some subjects keep better unopened.

Her voice was quieter than the doorway suggested it should be, precise rather than raised, each word set down like an instrument she'd already calibrated. It carried the particular weariness that comes of spending a career making catastrophic news sound like routine paperwork.

"Oh dear," she remarked. "You've brought evidence and emotion."\
Mrs Chain drew herself up. "I beg your pardon."\
"Not criticism," noted Fainrose. "Classification."

She glanced at the module again. "Come in," she instructed. "If that thing is active, the hallway is the worst possible place for it unless you happen to dislike your staircase."

They entered. The house smelled of paper, tea, solder, and intellectual impatience. Books lined every wall in layers of active use. A long table beneath the window held microscopes, mugs, and what Lolly realised was half of a dismantled detector array.

Mrs Chain surveyed the room. "She lives properly."\
"I make allowances for entropy," said Fainrose, closing the door.

Beatrix removed the module from Lolly's hands and placed it on the table. Fainrose looked at it for perhaps two seconds. Then she pronounced, "Idiots."

Lolly, who had spent the day surrounded by euphemism, felt an immediate and unreasonable surge of gratitude. "You know what it is," she managed.

"Yes," Fainrose confirmed. "A domestic projection stabiliser, third-generation civilian adaptation, vulgar casing, incompetent thermal shielding. I warned them not to deploy these into lived environments without local phase damping. They said I was being precious."

"Were you."\
"No," Fainrose replied. "I was being exact. Precision merely looks emotional to the incompetent, who prefer it in bullet points."

Jago wandered towards the bookshelves. "Did you scratch the note."\
"Of course."

Mrs Chain stepped forward. "My house has been replaced."\
Fainrose looked at her then, properly, and the severity of her face altered by a fraction. "Not replaced," she corrected. "Compressed. There's a difference."\
"I don't care for either."\
"No," replied Fainrose. "Most sane people don't."

She picked up the module with thumb and forefinger, carried it to a bench at the far end of the room, and placed it inside a wire mesh box connected to several cables. A screen above the bench lit at once with graphs, matrices, and a pulsing red bar marked

> LOCAL COERCION FIELD

Lolly took an involuntary half-step closer. Fainrose noticed without looking up. "You studied physics once."\
Lolly blinked. "How do you know that."\
"You're standing like a woman being insulted by notation."\
"That seems unfairly accurate."\
"Yes," Fainrose agreed. "The world rarely improves under scrutiny."

She tapped several keys. The red bar lengthened, then lengthened again, then split into several thinner lines that rose and fell like competing heartbeats. Fainrose stared at them. "Oh, that's worse than I expected."

Beatrix folded her arms. "How worse."

Fainrose pointed at the screen. "This device is not merely enforcing one stable domestic reading. It's selecting from multiple possible home-states and driving the property towards whichever one best satisfies central continuity metrics."

Mrs Chain demanded, "In English."\
"It is choosing the cheapest version of your life that still looks enough like your life to survive complaint."

Mrs Chain inhaled once, slowly, in the dangerous manner of a woman considering whether murder may briefly count as tidying.

Lolly pointed out, "But it isn't settling cleanly. The clocks, photographs, labels: they contradict one another."\
"Of course they do."

Fainrose considered her for a moment. "What's the difference, Miss Wren, between a state and a solution."\
Lolly opened her mouth, shut it again, and thought about it properly this time. "A state is just --- where something is. A solution is where it's supposed to end up."\
"Nearly." It was the closest thing to approval her face appeared built to produce. "A solution is where something stops moving. Most real things don't stop. They evolve. Your Ministry has confused a mid-sentence with a full stop."

She turned from the screen. "When a system evolves naturally," she explained, "it doesn't jump from richness to paperwork. Between measurements, a state changes by rule: smoothly, reversibly, deterministically, right down to the decimal. It's called unitary evolution. The law that runs it is [the Schrödinger equation](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=P__dEWo_WiI), and it is, without exception, the most law-abiding piece of physics you will ever meet. Nothing about it is random. Nothing about it is negotiable. They're imposing an end state without understanding the dynamics that produce one."

Lolly felt the sentence land with more force than the room had probably intended. "You mean---"\
"Yes," she confirmed. "They're vandalising the evolution."

Beatrix frowned. "Evolution of what."\
"Of everything that hasn't yet become final," Fainrose answered. "Homes. Routes. Identities. Intentions. Any state with more than one live continuation."\
Jago, still scanning titles on the shelves, said, "That sounds broad."\
"It is broad," Fainrose agreed. "Which is why one grows tired."

Behind her, the wire mesh box gave a small tick --- the sound a machine makes when it quietly changes its mind about something. No one else seemed to hear it.

Lolly had taken out the green notebook without noticing. Now she wrote: Not state alone. Need law of change.

Fainrose saw this and gave the smallest of nods. "Good," she noted. "At least one of you is house-trained."\
Mrs Chain looked between them. "What exactly are they doing to my house."

Fainrose paused, weighing how much truth a person holding an umbrella like a weapon ought to receive at once. "Imagine your home," she began, "not as a place, but as a cloud of possible details held together by habit, memory, objects, routes through rooms, repeated actions, and expectation."

Mrs Chain stared. "That is a surprisingly poetic start."\
"Don't interrupt. It won't happen again."

Fainrose continued. "Physicists have a word for that cloud, if you want it: [superposition](#glSuperposition). Ministries have their own word: backlog. Now imagine some administrative imbecile decides this complexity is inefficient. They force it into a reduced pattern selected for stability and cost. They don't understand which details support the rest, so they flatten the system before it has finished cohering."

Mrs Chain considered this. "In other words," she concluded, "they've forced in a bad substitute."\
"Yes."\
"Of something they did not understand."\
"Yes."\
"While being certain."\
"Yes."\
Mrs Chain nodded. "Animals."

Fainrose almost smiled. It was a quick, dangerous thing, gone before it could become sociable.

Beatrix asked, "Can it be reversed."\
"Locally, perhaps. Systemically, not by amateurs."\
"We did not come to you because we enjoy amateurs," said Beatrix.\
"No," Fainrose returned. "You came because someone at last noticed the Ministry has confused measurement with authority."

Lolly looked up. "That's what the queue was doing."\
"Obviously."\
"It was selecting the interpretive frame first and forcing the case into it."\
"Yes."\
"Then the house module does the same thing, materially."\
"Yes."\
"And the whole Simplification Initiative is---"\
"A badly governed experiment in coercive collapse," Fainrose finished. "Congratulations. You've almost caught up with your own morning."

Fainrose's face changed. Not much. Just enough. "The module," she murmured. "Thermal shielding. I told them it was ornamental." She tapped the descending line as if a domestic fault might still confess.

Beatrix saw the label before Fainrose did. "What."\
Fainrose leaned closer. The line was no longer pretending to be local. "I was wrong," she admitted, quietly. "That is not domestic."

Lolly joined her at the bench. A label had appeared beneath the descending line:

> REMOTE ANCHOR LOSS
>
> GRAVITATIONAL CIVIC MASS
>
> INDEX BELOW TOLERANCE

She read it twice. Then again.

"Gravitational civic mass?" she read aloud.\
Fainrose rubbed the bridge of her nose. "There are days," she sighed, "when I miss private thought."\
Mrs Chain came round the table. "What does it mean."

Fainrose called up a satellite map, then a network diagram overlaid with municipal infrastructure. At the centre of one regional cluster blinked a small black icon. Next to it, in yellow: Singularity Asset 4C Status: Diminishing

Jago put down the book he had been pretending to browse. "Oh," he said. "That seems bad."\
Fainrose was very still now. "Yes," she agreed. "It does."\
Lolly looked from the screen to her face. "What is Singularity Asset 4C."\
She answered without turning. "A black hole."

No one spoke. This was partly because the statement had arrived with the calm administrative weight of a tea tray, and partly because each of them required a moment to resent the universe properly.

Mrs Chain asked at last, "Why is there a black hole in a civic cluster."

Fainrose's mouth tightened. "Transport curvature, waste density management, long-range compression, several profoundly stupid infrastructure efficiencies from fifteen years ago. The Ministry likes gravity when it can invoice it."

Beatrix's eyes had the particular narrowness that meant she was already drafting a memo. "And who signed for it."\
"Fifteen consecutive junior ministers," supplied Fainrose, "each initialling a document they were assured someone senior had read."

"Why is it diminishing," Beatrix asked.\
"That," replied Fainrose, "is the question."

Lolly looked again at the falling line on the screen. "Can black holes diminish."\
Fainrose gave her a sidelong look. "Yes."\
"In a municipal timeframe?"\
"That," she admitted, "would be unusual enough to ruin my afternoon."

Jago leaned against the doorframe. "Would it be the sort of thing one ignores until it devours a county."\
"No," corrected Fainrose. "Worse."\
He frowned. "Worse than devouring a county."

"It may be evaporating."

Nobody spoke for a moment. Lolly heard the sentence not as sound but as a rearrangement of threat.

Jago broke the silence first, because someone always had to. "Is that better or worse than incoming."\
"A black hole," Lolly ventured carefully, "cannot simply evaporate."

Fainrose looked at her with almost maternal disappointment. "Oh, Lolly" --- it was the first time she had used her name --- "of course it can. The only question is whether someone has been foolish enough to make it do so where the public can object."

Mrs Chain gripped the umbrella. "I object already," she declared.\
"Quite right," Fainrose agreed.

She shut down the domestic display and isolated the black-hole trace in a narrow window of its own.

"We will leave Mrs Chain's house for the moment," she decided. "Not because it is unimportant. Because it is now evidence of a larger stupidity. If they have begun forcing collapse locally and interfering with gravitational anchors centrally, then the system is no longer merely rude. It is unstable."

She glanced once more at the falling index. "And whatever is doing it isn't slowing down."\
Beatrix nodded once. "Next step."\
Fainrose looked at all of them in turn. "Tea."\
Jago brightened. "At last. A response proportionate to the danger."

"Then," Fainrose continued, "we begin by assuming the worst, and we try, with discipline, not to underestimate it. Nobody in this room controls what that thing does next. We proceed regardless."

On the darkened screen behind her, unwatched, the index dropped one further point.

Mrs Chain set down the umbrella with ceremony. "At last," she pronounced, "someone sensible."

Lolly opened the green notebook and wrote four words. Fainrose not reassured. Beneath them, after a moment, she added two more: Neither am I. She closed the notebook without waiting to feel any braver about it, and stood up anyway.

Rule: Quantum theory is not strange because nothing obeys a law; it is strange because the law is so orderly until a record is made.

Lesson for this chapter: [Lawful Evolution Between Measurements](#lesson-5-lawful-evolution)

# **Chapter 6: The Object Is Not Mislaid**

Stephanie Fainrose remarked there was a black [hole](#glBlackHole) in regional infrastructure while adjusting the flame under a kettle with one hand and isolating a collapsing gravitational trace with the other.

The kitchen smelled of scorched flux and Earl Grey --- the two ingredients, Lolly had privately decided, from which Dr Fainrose was assembled.

Nobody spoke for several seconds. Then Jago Flint said, "I don't wish to seem needlessly difficult, but that sounds like the sort of thing someone ought to have mentioned on a sign. In block capitals. Possibly laminated."

Fainrose ignored him. On the narrow screen above the wire mesh box, the line marked Singularity Asset 4C continued its steady descent: not plunging, not jittering, not failing theatrically, but diminishing with the calm self-possession of a process obeying a law rather than filing an incident report.

Lolly found this more disturbing than panic would have been. She moved closer to the display. "When you say black hole---"\
"I do," Fainrose replied.\
"Yes, but in what sense."\
"In the astrophysical one, regrettably."

Mrs Elspeth Chain, who had accepted over the last day that government could ruin a home, falsify a queue, and rename a sister out of existence, still seemed to feel that black holes ought to sit above the civil-service grade at which incompetence became infrastructure.

"You cannot possibly mean," she managed, "that the Ministry put an actual cosmic abyss under public administration."

Fainrose poured water into a teapot. "My dear Mrs Chain," she remarked, "the modern state will administer anything it cannot properly understand. Gravity is attractive to them because it looks authoritative from a distance."

Beatrix Sloan remained by the bench, arms folded. "You observed transport curvature and waste density management."\
"Yes."\
"That sounds invented."\
"Almost everything important does, at first hearing."

She brought the tea to the table with the composure of a woman whose threshold for disaster had long ago ceased to be numerical. "There were experimental civic singularities fifteen years ago," she explained. "Tiny, engineered, heavily contained, used as anchor points for compression arrays, freight deflection, deep storage, and several proposals too idiotic to survive daylight. Most were decommissioned, on paper. A few, I suspect, were transferred into administrative twilight, where oversight goes when ministers require deniability."

Nobody asked how she came to know the arrangement in such tidy detail. Former Ministry physicists tended to know exactly this kind of thing, and asking would only have confirmed it.

Jago accepted a cup. "And this one."\
"This one," answered Fainrose, "appears to be shrinking."

The reading updated itself again before anyone could comment on it, then again, each number arriving a little sooner than the last.

Lolly sat down without entirely deciding to. "Black holes can lose mass," she considered slowly. "In principle."

Fainrose looked at her over the rim of her spectacles. "Yes."\
"But not because someone moved a decimal point."\
"No."\
"Then why is it happening now."

Fainrose set down her cup and returned to the bench. With quick, exact movements she called up a second plot beside the first: one curve descending, another climbing with glacial patience.

> TEMPERATURE ESTIMATE

"Because," she noted, "the universe is under no obligation to preserve large, gloomy objects merely because institutions have built paperwork around them."

Lolly stood again. "You think it's [Hawking radiation](#glHawking)."

"I think," Fainrose replied, "that you already suspect the answer, or you would not have stood up. Watch the two curves and tell me what mass does to temperature."

Mrs Chain stared. "Who is Hawking."

There was a silence. Beatrix closed her eyes briefly.

Jago clarified, "A physicist, Mrs Chain, not a department."\
"Though his name does have the regrettable competence of one," Fainrose added.

Lolly was watching the screen. "That line, is that temperature increasing."\
"Yes."\
"And the downward line is mass."\
"Yes."\
"So the smaller it gets, the hotter it becomes."\
"Yes."\
"And eventually---"

"Eventually," Fainrose finished, "the object ceases to be a useful noun. The rate is not constant. It is accelerating, which is a technical way of saying you should not relax."

Jago sipped tea. "I dislike it when physics becomes literary."\
"It is a flaw in the subject," Fainrose agreed.

Beatrix stepped closer to the display. "How certain."\
"Of the mechanism, moderately. Of the consequences, sufficiently."\
"That is not a percentage."\
"No."

Fainrose adjusted the scale again. The descending curve sharpened at its lower end, as though modestly reluctant to admit what came next. "There is a vulgar public explanation," she continued, "involving particle pairs at the horizon, one escaping while the other falls in. It is not wholly useless, but it encourages exactly the wrong sort of confidence: people picture two little billiard balls and feel they have understood something. The cleaner statement, and the one that is actually computed, is that a quantum field does not sit politely empty in curved spacetime; you sum every history the field could have and weight each by its own small rotating arrow, and what comes out at infinity is thermal. Mr Feynman built an entire, perfectly serious theory out of adding up arrows for paths no sensible person would take, and it kept being right, which he found nearly as irritating as I do. Once you accept that the bookkeeping is the physics, the rest is book-keeping with terrible implications."

Lolly opened the green notebook. "Thermal," she noted aloud while writing. "So not random debris. Not leakage. Radiation with a temperature."\
"Quite."\
"And if it radiates, it loses energy."\
"Yes."\
"And since mass and energy---"\
"Are old friends," Fainrose finished.

She did not say how expensive old friendships could get. On the screen, the number was already doing the arithmetic for her.

She wrote faster.

Mrs Chain, who had no intention of being excluded merely because the apocalypse had become mathematical, demanded, "What happens when it's gone."

Nobody answered her at once. Outside, something metallic shifted in the garden. A branch tapped the window with the faint insistence of weather wishing to be kept informed.

At last Fainrose offered, "That depends on what the Ministry has attached to it."\
Beatrix's expression hardened. "Anchors."\
"Yes."\
"Compression fields."\
"Yes."\
"Transport shortcuts."\
"Possibly."\
Mrs Chain set down her cup with care. "In ordinary language."

Fainrose turned. "If they built civic systems around a singularity," she explained, "then those systems are using extreme gravity as part of their structure. Remove the object, and whatever was leaning on it must either fail, detach, or seek another way to remain possible."

Jago gave this a moment's thought. "Roads may become philosophical."\
"Roads are already philosophical," Lolly countered.\
"Good," Jago decided. "Then they may begin issuing manifestos at roundabouts."

Fainrose almost smiled again.

Lolly looked from the plots to the wire-mesh box holding the domestic stabiliser. "The house module found it."\
"Yes."\
"Why?"

"Because the same idiots are involved," Fainrose stated. "Coercive civic simplification requires anchors. If you want to force rich systems into cheaper states, you need somewhere for the surplus possibility to go."

Mrs Chain blinked. "I'm sorry. The surplus what."

"Possibility," Fainrose continued. "Ambiguity. Unrealised structure. Contradictory detail. The things bureaucracies call inefficiency when they mean life."

Lolly stopped writing. A thought had arrived with the unpleasant smoothness of a blade entering water. "You mean the Ministry hasn't just been classifying reality," she pressed. "It has been removing degrees of freedom and dumping the cost elsewhere."\
Fainrose held her gaze. "Yes."\
"Into the singularity."\
"Likely."

Nobody in the kitchen moved. Somewhere behind her a cable connector ticked once as it cooled, and for one full second it was the loudest thing in the room.

Jago set his cup down. "And now the singularity is evaporating."\
"Yes."\
"So all the suppressed nonsense comes back out."\
Fainrose tilted her head. "That is not a scientific phrase."\
"Is it wrong."\
She considered. "No," she allowed. "Annoyingly."

Beatrix spoke at once. "Then we need location, rate, and threshold."\
"I'm getting them."\
"And after that."

Fainrose's fingers paused over the keyboard. "After that," she replied, "we determine whether this is merely catastrophic, or historically embarrassing."

Lolly stared at the temperature curve. There was a peculiar stillness to the room now, the stillness produced when every person present has understood the same fact but not yet found a shape in which to admit it. The black hole was not being stolen. It was not being hidden. It was not awaiting approval. It was vanishing because the laws of quantum physics, after years of patient neglect, had at last presented their invoice.

Fainrose did not look away from the screen. "Nobody has been watching this," she murmured, half to herself. "No observer, no clever collapse forced by a curious eye. It has simply been sitting in the dark, doing what physics does whether or not anyone is checking its paperwork. That is rather the point I keep failing to get across to this Ministry. Reality does not require an audience. It only requires enough time."

Mrs Chain broke the silence first. "Can it be stopped."

Fainrose did not answer immediately, which was answer enough to be offensive. "Perhaps slowed," she offered at last. "Not by people who say leveraging gravitational assets in meetings."\
"Can it be repaired."\
"One does not repair a black hole, Mrs Chain. One regrets having treated it municipally."

Jago let out a quiet breath. "Marvellous sentence."\
Beatrix ignored him. "How long."

Fainrose expanded the end of the curve until the graph showed only a steepening tail. "That," she noted, "depends on its actual mass, its present feed rate, and how aggressively they have been using it as a sink for administrative overreach. Large black holes [evaporate so slowly as to be practically eternal](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=cFslUSyfZPc). Small ones are another matter."

The room went very quiet at that, the specific quiet of several people doing the same arithmetic and not liking where it landed.

Beatrix pressed, "A number, Fainrose."\
"A bad one," Fainrose replied. "Single-digit hours, if the feed rate holds steady. Fewer, if it doesn't."\
Lolly looked up. "And this one is small."

"It was designed to be manageable," noted Fainrose, in a tone making plain that this was among the more reckless adjectives ever attached to a singularity, just ahead of affordable.

The kettle clicked off behind them. Somewhere in the shelves, a clock chose that moment to resume, giving a single abrupt click as though time itself had remembered an appointment.

Lolly turned another page in the notebook. "If the radiation is thermal," she considered, "then what comes out doesn't obviously tell you what fell in."

Fainrose's face went rigid. "No," she admitted.\
Jago glanced between them. "I don't care for that tone."\
"You shouldn't," Fainrose agreed.\
Beatrix ordered, "Explain."

Fainrose rested both hands on the bench. "If a black hole forms from real matter, absorbs structure, and later evaporates into radiation that appears to carry only broad thermodynamic character, then one must ask what became of the [original information](#glInformation)."

Mrs Chain repeated, "Information."\
"Yes."\
"Not matter."\
"Both, if you enjoy losing sleep properly."

"For what it is worth," Fainrose added, "nobody has actually caught the universe deleting anything yet. Most of my colleagues would bet the information is still out there somewhere, smuggled out in correlations too fine for this equipment to read. But 'somewhere' is doing a great deal of unpaid overtime in that sentence."

Lolly felt a chill quite unrelated to temperature. "The house," she breathed.

Fainrose nodded once. "The house."\
"The queue."\
"Yes."\
"The missing details. The erased alternatives. The parts they compressed."\
"Yes, Lolly."

She stared at the shrinking curve and understood, with a sensation bordering on nausea, that the Ministry's project was not merely untidy governance. It was a wager against the recoverability of reality.

Mrs Chain rose from the table. "I knew," she pronounced, "the moment they altered my crockery that this would end in moral sewage." No one contradicted her. She did not ask whether any of it could be undone. She had stopped expecting things to be undone somewhere around Tuesday, and had found, rather to her own surprise, that this made her considerably harder to stop.

Fainrose typed three final commands. A printer beneath the bench woke, considered its obligations, and began producing a long sheet of dense numbers and clipped warnings. At the top, in red, appeared:

> SINGULARITY ASSET 4C
>
> NET MASS LOSS CONSISTENT WITH HAWKING REGIME ANCHOR
>
> INSTABILITY ESCALATING

Jago read the heading over Fainrose's shoulder. "Asset 4C," he noted. "Of course it is."\
Beatrix took the page as it emerged. "Good," she declared. "Now we can prove they knew."

Fainrose removed her spectacles, cleaned them once, and put them back on. "No," she corrected. "Now we can prove they should have known. Which, in institutional terms, is often the more fatal category."

Jago stood up. "I'm beginning to like science. It keeps its vindictive edges properly peer-reviewed."\
Mrs Chain picked up her umbrella. "And what exactly do we do now."

Fainrose looked towards the window, where the afternoon had become unnaturally bright in the flat administrative way weather sometimes acquires before events. "We go and see," she decided, "how close the country is to being held together by an evaporating hole and several assumptions."

Lolly closed the notebook and, for once, did not open it again to check she had got the wording right. She stood. On the last line she had written: Not vanishing by error. Vanishing by law. Then, after a moment: Much worse.

Fainrose turned to the gravitational trace and then to Lolly. "We have a clock," she informed her. "A real one. Not a Ministry deadline. The anchor is evaporating, and when it goes, whatever is still inside it stops being recoverable."

Lolly wrote the time down and underlined it twice. For once, the paperwork was not the most frightening thing in the room.

Rule: Asset 4C is not going missing; it is becoming hotter, smaller, and harder to forgive.

Lesson for this chapter: [Hawking Radiation](#lesson-6-hawking-radiation)

# **Chapter 7: What Remains Correlated**

By the time they reached the Regional Compression Annex, the day had acquired the exhausted brightness peculiar to national mistakes. The building stood behind two fences, one camera mast, and a sign which read:

> CIVIC INFRASTRUCTURE HARMONISATION SITE

Authorised access only

No public gravitation beyond this point

"There," said Jago Flint, slowing the car, "is a sentence nobody wrote with a clear conscience or a functioning solicitor."

The annex had once been a distribution hub, though years of government improvement had polished it into the sort of place that no longer admitted to having had a function. Its walls were pale, windowless, and faintly curved in a way that suggested either modern design or local damage to geometry. Several service roads led towards it and then, halfway there, seemed to lose confidence and become lay-bys.

Fainrose observed all this without visible surprise. "That's new," she remarked.\
"The building?" asked Lolly.\
"The curvature suppression."

She looked again. Now that she had noticed it, the air above the concrete yard did seem oddly mannered, as if distances were being encouraged not to express themselves fully.

Beatrix had already stepped out of the car and was walking towards the gate with the clipped inevitability of an approaching audit.

"Do we have entry authority," Lolly asked.\
"No," Beatrix replied.\
"Then what exactly is the plan."\
"I am going to ask in a tone that makes refusal sound career-limiting and pension-adjacent."

Mrs Chain emerged from the back seat with her umbrella and surveyed the sign. "No public gravitation," she read. "They always phrase wickedness as if it were a leisure rule."

The gate guard, whose relationship with sleep appeared to have become historical, took one look at Beatrix's identification, one look at Fainrose's face, and decided that regulations were excellent in principle but perhaps not in this specific hour. They were admitted.

His hand drifted for a moment towards the phone on the gatehouse wall, weighed how many superiors were worth waking at this hour, and thought better of all of them.

Inside, the annex was colder than the day required. Corridors extended in several directions under long white lights, labelled with the sort of terminology institutions use when trying to make dangerous machinery sound recyclable: VECTOR SANITISATION, METRIC STORAGE, ANCHOR MANAGEMENT. One arrow had been hastily covered over, but not so well that Lolly could not still read GRAVITY WELL OBSERVATION beneath the tape.

It smelled of photocopier toner and hot electronics, and beneath that something colder and more mineral, a faint ozone tang Lolly half-recognised from a school science demonstration and spent the rest of the day failing to place anywhere reassuring.

The tape itself had already started to lift at one corner and curl away from the sign, the way tape does in rooms that cannot settle on a temperature.

"Subtle," Jago noted.\
"It used to be worse," Fainrose admitted.\
"You've been here."\
"Once. I resigned shortly afterwards."

This seemed fair.

"Did you know," Jago mused, apropos of nothing, "that a man I met at a conference is entirely convinced cryptocurrency prices are driven by quantum tunnelling?"\
Fainrose did not break stride. "People believe a great many things are driven by quantum tunnelling. None of them are cryptocurrency."\
"He had graphs."\
"People with graphs," Fainrose replied, "are the ones I trust least of all."

"And yet," Fainrose added, a few steps later, in the tone of someone constitutionally unable to leave well enough alone, "the universe does keep a currency of its own. Planck's constant. Physicists call it, without any irony whatsoever, the quantum of action --- the smallest denomination anything is ever issued in. You cannot spend half of one, any more than a shopkeeper can hand you half a penny and call it change."\
"So the crypto man was almost right."\
"The crypto man," Fainrose corrected, "was not right about anything. He simply happened to be standing near something true."

They passed through two security doors, a control station, and a room full of humming cabinets attended by three technicians who possessed the unmistakable air of people who had obeyed instructions for so long that reality itself now felt above their pay grade.

On the far wall, a large display showed the same descending trace they had seen at Fainrose's house, only here it was surrounded by status lights, internal bulletins, and an amber warning banner that read:

> ANCHOR THERMAL DRIFT WITHIN PROJECTED ENVELOPE

No intervention required at this time

Status: nominal (redefined)

"Within projected envelope," Lolly read, and thought of every envelope she had ever been handed that contained a problem larger than the paper. Projected, as a word, had begun to mean "we have drawn a line and would prefer the universe to colour inside it."

Fainrose stopped dead. "Oh," she noted, softly. "Now I'm angry."

One of the technicians rose. He was young, eager, and had the brittle optimism of someone convinced that complex systems were improved by badges. "This area is restricted," he began.

Beatrix placed the printed diagnostic from Fainrose's bench on the console. "No," she corrected, "this area is evidence."

He looked down at the page, then up at Fainrose, and visibly regretted several earlier life choices. "We've been assured," he said, "that the anchor is shedding excess load in a controlled manner."

Fainrose stared at him. "That sentence," she informed him, "contains so little understanding I scarcely know where to begin."

Lolly moved closer to the master display. Several subsidiary windows were open around the main mass-loss curve. One showed municipal transport routes blinking in and out like doubtful constellations. Another listed compression burdens by district. A third, to her growing unease, contained a stream of domestic reconciliation events, each with timestamps and area codes.

Mrs Chain saw it too. "My house," she breathed.

There it was.

> CHAIN, E.

Residential convergence: incomplete.

Residual identity conflict: elevated.

Overflow channel: accepted.

Lolly read the last line twice. Overflow channel.

She turned to Fainrose. "They really are using the singularity as a sink."

"Yes," Fainrose confirmed. "For unresolved alternatives, unstable classifications, excess correlation, and anything else their system cannot afford to keep locally."

The young technician tried a professional smile and failed at it midway. "It's only metadata."\
"No," Fainrose corrected. "That is what foolish people call reality when they are too cowardly to say they have abstracted it."

"Metadata," Jago repeated, as if tasting a word that had never done an honest day's work. "That's what you call the part that tells you who the letter was for, after you've put the letter in the wrong hole."

Beatrix was already scanning the local logs. "How long have they known about the thermal rise."\
The technician hesitated. Jago leaned on the console. "I ask this as a friend of institutional collapse: answer quickly."\
"Three months," he admitted.\
Beatrix looked up. "Three months."\
"It was judged non-critical."

Behind him, another console gave a soft, private chime: the kind meant for a screen nobody in the room was currently watching.

Fainrose gave a short laugh devoid of amusement. "Of course it was. Slow disasters are irresistible to committees. They offer so many opportunities for scheduling."

Beatrix's eyes stayed on the screen. Half-buried under a routing header sat a stamp not meant for her to find: OVERFLOW PROVISION: AUTHORISED UNDER PHASE TWO. She copied it into her own notebook without comment, then asked, mildly, "Who signs off on Phase Two."\
The technician blinked, as if the question had arrived in a language he'd once studied and let lapse. "Above my clearance," he admitted, relieved, for once, to be telling the truth.

Lolly was no longer looking at the technicians. She was watching the transport map. Each time one route vanished, a line elsewhere brightened. Each time a domestic event entered the overflow channel, the anchor field fluctuated by a tiny amount. Separately, none of it meant much. Together, it produced a pattern so offensively neat that she felt insulted on behalf of causality.

"These aren't separate failures," she declared.\
Fainrose glanced at her sideways. "Tell me what they are, then."

"They're coupled." Lolly declared it before she'd quite finished deciding whether she believed it, generally, she was starting to notice, the moment she turned out to be right. "The routes, the queue, the houses. Not failing separately. Failing together, because none of it was ever separate to begin with."

Fainrose came to stand beside her. "Yes."

On the lower part of the screen, hidden behind system tabs, was a label she had not noticed before:

> CORRELATION BUDGET

Beneath it, a meter was moving steadily towards red.

"What," Lolly asked, "is a correlation budget."

The room became quiet in the way rooms do when language has at last chosen to become incriminating.

Fainrose folded her arms. "It means that some administrative ghoul decided interconnectedness was too expensive to maintain in full."

Mrs Chain frowned. "Interconnectedness of what."\
"Lives. Records. Places. Choices. Shared histories. Mutual constraints. Any detail whose meaning depends on something else."

Jago considered this. "So instead of managing complexity, they've been charging it rent, with a late fee for sentiment."\
"Precisely."

The technician, perhaps sensing a need to defend the dead, ventured, "The system only suppresses non-essential linkages."

Mrs Chain turned to him calmly, like a woman who had already passed through outrage and was now travelling under clearer weather. "My sister," she stated, "is not a linkage." She did not raise her voice. She had simply stopped allowing the sentence to be true, and gone on standing there regardless.

Nobody improved on this.

Fainrose reached over the console and opened a deeper systems pane. The diagram that appeared looked less like software than a nervous breakdown rendered in geometry: district nodes, domestic nodes, routing lattices, and at the centre a black disk marked 4C, around which thousands of fine lines converged and vanished.

Beatrix studied it a moment longer. "Same reference," she noted. "That's the printout from Fainrose's kitchen. Everything in this building is plugged into the exact object that's dying." She filed it as evidence, not as feeling.

"They attached everything to it," Lolly pressed.\
"Not everything," Fainrose replied. "Only everything they meant to simplify."\
"And now it's evaporating."\
"Yes."\
"So the things tied to it don't just break. They become uncertain together."\
"Exactly."

One of the senior technicians entered at that moment, carrying a clipboard and a settled confidence that had survived many meetings by confusing sequence with thought. "What is going on here."\
Beatrix held up her identification. "Official review."\
"We have no notice of review."\
"You do now."

He was compact and precisely barbered, the sort of man who had never once left the house looking uncertain of anything, whatever else was true that morning --- which Lolly would come, in time, to find the most alarming thing about him.

He took this badly and looked to the monitors for institutional support. Instead he found Fainrose. His face altered. "Dr Fainrose."\
"Mr Voller."\
"I was told you had become private."\
"And I was told you had become harmless. We are both disappointed."

Voller drew himself up. "The anchor remains within tolerance."

Fainrose pointed at the red-trending mass curve. "No. The software remains within tolerance. The black hole is evaporating. We established that an hour ago, over tea."\
He smiled the smile of a career bureaucrat greeting an inconvenient noun. "That interpretation is contested."\
"It is thermodynamics," Fainrose stated. "Not a consultation, and not a re-run."

Lolly almost wrote that down and then realised she had already started.

Voller turned to the correlation display. "The coupling lattice is robust."\
Fainrose's expression became almost kind, which made it much worse. "Yes," she noted. "That is the problem. The more robust the entanglement, the less politely the decoherence spreads."\
Voller blinked. "The what spreads."

"The arrows," interjected Lolly, before Fainrose could answer. "The ones from yesterday."

Fainrose looked at her, briefly startled, then approving. "Yes. Nothing stops adding up, Mr Voller: that's the [superposition](#glSuperposition), and it does not care whether you find it agreeable. Decoherence is simpler and worse: something else (a gauge, a log, a filing cabinet) starts keeping its own record of which history happened. Once two records exist, the arrows can no longer be added back together. Nothing has collapsed. It has merely become impossible to forget, on your behalf, without your consent."

Now Lolly was listening with her whole body.

Fainrose tapped the central black disk. "You tied many local systems to one central quantum-gravitational anchor. They no longer have separate stories to tell; they share structure. Once the anchor becomes noisy, hot, or unstable, the environment starts learning which alternatives are linked to which, and once it knows, the arrows can no longer line up. Coherence drains away. The system stops behaving like one carefully maintained possibility and starts behaving like a classical mess."

The technician at the near console ventured, "That sounds speculative."\
"It sounds," Fainrose replied, "like you were never taught any quantum mechanics."

She turned to Lolly instead, since teaching the willing was less exhausting than converting the credentialed. "Go on, then. What have I just described?"

Lolly hesitated, abruptly aware that the whole room was about to find out whether she had understood any of this or had merely absorbed it.

Lolly looked back at the diagram. "[Entanglement](#glEntanglement). You can't describe the parts separately any more: not because you don't know enough, but because there's [no separate story left to know](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tuyY2RlseBM)."\
"And the anchor's noise?"\
"Decoherence. The environment starts keeping the record instead of you."\
"And once it's kept?"\
"The lost alternatives don't recombine cleanly." She hesitated. "They become records of damage."

Fainrose glanced at her, and for a moment there was something almost approving in her face. "That's the part everyone skips."

Mrs Chain surveyed the room, the screens, the men, the language, and the trembling civic skeleton of the thing. "In plain English," she demanded.

Fainrose obliged. "They have connected too many delicate realities to one dying object. Now that object is leaking order."\
"That," allowed Mrs Chain, "is plain enough."

On the main display, the correlation budget crossed into red. Every monitor in the room flickered once. Then again. Across the transport map, three routes vanished simultaneously. The domestic-events stream began scrolling too fast to read. Somewhere deeper in the annex, an alarm started and then stopped, as if embarrassed to have noticed reality before management had approved the event.

Voller turned pale. "What did you do."\
Fainrose did not move. "I arrived," she replied. "The universe did the rest."

Lolly was watching one smaller pane on the right-hand side of the screen. It listed overflow returns. Items that had been channelled into the anchor were beginning, for want of a better term, to come back. Not whole. Fragments. Alternative street names. Contradictory birth records. Old queue tokens. Ownership disputes. Duplicate memorial photographs. Rejected room layouts. Mislaid correspondences. A hundred small amputations of the ordinary, all pressing at once against the weakened civic membrane.

"They're coming out," she breathed.\
"They were never gone," Fainrose corrected. "They were only stored somewhere obscene."

Beatrix had already moved to the emergency controls. "Can we isolate districts."\
"Not without making choices," Fainrose replied.\
"What choices."\
"Which correlations to save."

Nobody enjoyed that sentence.

Jago looked at the map and whistled softly. "Well," he said, "we seem at last to have found the national character: underfunded entanglement with a modest contingency reserve."

Fainrose gave him a look that had ended arguments with better-funded physicists. "Must you."\
"It's a good word," replied Jago, entirely unrepentant. "Does a great deal of work for a noun."

Voller was shouting into a desk phone now, demanding senior clearance from people who were almost certainly already practising distance.

The screens flashed again. This time Lolly felt it: not physically, exactly, more a brief rearrangement in the logic of nearby things, as if every object in the room had been forced to remember what else it depended on. She saw Mrs Chain grip the umbrella, saw Beatrix brace one hand on the console, saw Jago step back from a monitor that had begun listing addresses in two different counties at once.

On the central display, a new warning appeared:

> ANCHOR COHERENCE LOSS CASCADING
>
> LOCAL RECOVERY NO LONGER COMMUTATIVE
>
> PROJECTED FULL DECORRELATION: UNDER THE HOUR, IF THE TREND HOLDS

Lolly stared at the last line, which had, unusually for this building, told the truth and hedged it in the same breath.

Fainrose saw it too. "Oh, excellent," she remarked without enthusiasm. "Order now matters. And apparently we're on a clock about it."

Mrs Chain objected, "I beg your pardon."

"It means," explained Lolly, before she could stop herself, "that you can't fix everything in any sequence you like. If two repairs don't commute, doing A then B gives a different result from doing B then A."

Fainrose looked at her. "Yes. And now you see why I never sleep."\
Beatrix turned from the controls. "Can we still stop the cascade."\
Fainrose's answer was immediate. "No."

The room held that. Then she added, "But we may still choose its shape. We don't get to decide whether the thing happens: only what we do while it does. That's most of what a life is."

That was the point, Lolly realised, at which disaster ceased to be theoretical and became administration again, though of a more honest and terrible sort, the kind where nobody could hide behind the paperwork any longer, because the paperwork was what was failing.

Beatrix straightened. "Right," she decided. "Then we prioritise."
