Mrs Chain lifted her umbrella. "At last," she pronounced, "a meeting with a moral centre." She did not ask whether it would work. She had stopped, Lolly noticed, asking the questions only fear could answer, and started asking the ones that led somewhere.

Fainrose began issuing instructions so quickly that even panic had to become organised to keep up. "Freeze new convergence events. Cut overflow routing. Detach non-essential transport couplings. Preserve hospitals, archives, and power lattices. Anything sentimental with structural dependency goes above policy threshold."

Voller stared. "Sentimental."

"Yes," snapped Fainrose. "That is where people keep the irreplaceable pieces, Mr Voller. We have been over this as a civilisation."

Behind her, the projected countdown had already lost four minutes to the argument.

By the time Voller had rerouted the last of the overflow couplings, the countdown had stopped falling. Nobody announced it. The room simply noticed, some minutes later, that they had stopped flinching at the display.

Lolly opened her notebook to a clean page and wrote:

Entangled systems fail together. Decohered systems do not forgive sequence.

Then, beneath it:

The country is attached to a dying argument with physics.

Rule: When connections are treated as expendable, a shared quantum state shares the damage as well.

Lesson for this chapter: [Entanglement and the Start of Decoherence](#lesson-7-entanglement-and-decoherence)

# **Chapter 8: The Backup That Could Not Exist**

Mr Voller had reached this stage by twenty past four. "There is a restoration protocol," he announced.

The Regional Compression Annex had by then acquired the atmosphere of a hospital in which the patient had begun offering opinions. Two of the wall displays were showing districts that did not currently agree on their own boundaries. A printer in the corner had produced eleven copies of a form that had never been designed. Somewhere below them, in the deeper part of the building where the anchor housing sat behind three doors and a good deal of optimism, something was making a sound not unlike a cathedral clearing its throat.

Nobody had explained it. Nobody, Lolly noticed, had gone to look.

Somewhere above the false ceiling, rain had started again, audible only as a faint, patient drumming nobody in the room had time to notice.

Stephanie Fainrose did not look up from the console. "Say that again," she instructed.

Voller straightened his cuffs, which is what such men do instead of praying, having long ago misfiled the latter as an external dependency. "There is a restoration protocol," he repeated. "Full-state civic reconstruction. We hold a reference copy."

Beatrix Sloan turned very slowly. "You hold what."

Her pen, which had not moved once in the last hour, began, almost imperceptibly, to tap against the console. Nobody but Lolly seemed to notice.

"A reference copy," Voller explained. "Of the pre-simplification configuration. Homes, records, routes, identity linkages. Everything the Initiative touched was mirrored before adjustment. It was a condition of approval. I signed the assurance myself."

"Whose condition." Beatrix did not look up; it was the tone of a woman filing something away for later.\
Voller hesitated. "It came down from above," he said. "I countersigned. I did not originate it."\
"I know every clerk in this building by their tea order, Mr Voller," Jago noted. "I have never once met whoever writes the conditions."

Lolly, who had spent the last hour watching reality itemise its own injuries, felt a brief, treacherous flare of hope. It lasted as long as it took to look at Fainrose's face, which had gone quite still.

"Mr Voller," she managed, and her voice had lost all its edges, which was infinitely worse than when it had them, "where is this copy."

"Cold vault. Sub-level two. Independent substrate, isolated field, no live coupling." He permitted himself the smallest recovery of dignity. "Precisely so that it could not be affected by anything occurring above."

"And what," Fainrose asked, "is in it."\
Voller blinked. "The state. The prior state."\
"Which prior state."\
"The correct one."\
Fainrose closed her eyes for a moment. "Lolly," she called. "Come here."

She went.

She pulled a fresh pane onto the display and typed a short command. The vault manifest opened: page after page of entries, each one a district, a street, a dwelling, a person, a linkage, all neatly indexed, all timestamped, all marked with a small green tick indicating successful capture.

It was, Lolly thought, one of the most reassuring documents she had ever seen, and she understood at once that this was the problem with it.

"Look at the fidelity column," Fainrose instructed.

She looked.

> STATE CAPTURE: COMPLETE
>
> CLASSICAL REPRESENTATION: VERIFIED
>
> PHASE RECORD: NOT APPLICABLE
>
> Lolly had seen "not applicable" used to mean "we did not measure it," "we lost it," and "we would rather you stopped asking." This was the first time she had seen it used to mean "the laws of physics sent their apologies."

She read the last line three times, in the manner of a woman hoping that words might be persuaded. "Not applicable."\
"Not applicable," agreed Fainrose. "They didn't record the phase relations."

"That," Jago said, "is a remarkably expensive way of not answering a question." Nobody laughed. It was, Lolly thought, the least funny thing he had said all day, which was itself informative.

Lolly went still. She had used that phrase herself an hour before, three floors up, about a dying anchor shedding its alternatives into an overflow channel. Meeting it again down here, dressed as a filing procedure, was disconcerting. "They could not record the phase relations," she considered slowly. "Then this isn't a copy."\
"No," Fainrose replied. "It is a photograph of a conversation, with all the inconvenient replies removed."

Mrs Elspeth Chain had come to the console with her umbrella in the crook of her arm and the expression of a woman determined to be present at the moment when the men admitted it. "Explain," she instructed.

Fainrose turned to her, and something in her manner changed. She spoke to Mrs Chain, always, as though Mrs Chain were the only person in the room whose understanding actually mattered, which, Lolly had begun to suspect, was a fairly accurate assessment.

"When they measured your house," Fainrose began, "in order to save it, they had to ask it questions."\
"They asked a great many."\
"Yes. And each question returned an answer. Blue wallpaper. One duvet. Husband present. Sister absent. All perfectly recordable. All faithfully written down." She tapped the manifest. "That is what is in the vault. Answers."\
"And that is not the house."\
"That is not the house. The house was also the relations between the answers: the way the sister depended on the kitchen, and the kitchen on the route to the shops, and the route on forty years of walking it."

"Those relations have a name," Fainrose went on, "and it is not a flattering one to a filing system. Phase. Picture it the way a better physicist than anyone in this building once did: every possible way your questionnaire could have gone carries its own small rotating arrow, and nothing becomes real until you add the arrows tip to tail and see what is left standing. Length tells you how likely an outcome is. Angle (phase) tells you whether two possibilities reinforce one another or cancel out on the way to becoming a house. Ask for a length and you get an honest number. Ask for an angle and the arrow stops spinning, because asking has forced it to stand still. I told you once already that the bookkeeping is the physics. That is what your vault could not put in a column, Mr Voller. Not the answer. The angle it was standing at."

Below them, on cue, the sound did not repeat. The quiet that followed it was the least reassuring thing Lolly had heard so far.

Mrs Chain considered this for some time. "So they could have one or the other," she concluded.\
"Yes."\
"But not both."\
"Not both. Never both. Not by incompetence. By law."

Fainrose's mouth tightened. "And so, being administrators, they took the one that fitted the form, and called the other one an acceptable loss."

Jago, from his position against the doorframe, ventured, "Forgive me. Could they not simply have fashioned a second house and asked it the other question."\
"No," Fainrose replied.\
"Why not."\
"Because [you cannot copy it](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=JhHMJCUmq28)\
, Mr Flint. Not won't. Cannot. That is [the no-cloning theorem](#glNocloning)\
, and it does not care how urgently you need it to be otherwise."

Voller engineered a small noise of protest. "We back up systems constantly. Every department---"\
"You back up records," Fainrose corrected. "You have never once backed up a state. Nobody has. Nobody will. If a general quantum state could be duplicated, you could measure the copy without disturbing the original, and the entire structure of physics would come apart in your hands like wet cardboard. It cannot be done. It has been known that it cannot be done for a very long time. It is in the literature. It is not obscure." She looked at him. "You signed an assurance that you had done an impossible thing, Mr Voller, and nobody in the approval chain knew enough to laugh."

The silence that followed had a specific quality: five people arriving, independently and at the same moment, at a conclusion nobody wanted to be first to say aloud.

Lolly broke it. "Then the vault is worthless."

"No," Fainrose replied. "Worthless would be a mercy. The vault is plausible. Which means that at some point today, a senior person will be told that a full restoration is available, and that person will authorise it, and the system will overwrite what remains of the real configuration with a flattened cartoon of it, and it will report success in green letters."

The display did not wait to be proven right: a small window opened beside the manifest, official, tidy, already counting.

> REQUEST: FULL-STATE RESTORATION (RCA)
>
> REQUESTED BY: OFFICE OF THE DUTY DIRECTOR
>
> AWAITING: ONE SENIOR SIGNATURE
>
> WINDOW CLOSES: 00:09:41

Beatrix had already moved. "Then we lock the vault."\
"Yes," Fainrose agreed.\
"On what authority."\
"Mine," Beatrix replied. "Retroactively."

Voller found his voice. "You cannot seal a national restoration asset on a theoretical objection."\
Beatrix looked at him with the particular patience of an auditor who has been waiting eleven years for exactly this sentence. "Mr Voller," she informed him, "I have sealed a hospital laundry contract over a missing signature. You have no idea what I am capable of when the physics is on my side. It is an unusually well-documented side."

"I have known theoretical objections outlive entire ministries," Jago observed, to no one in particular.

She began typing. Below them, the cathedral cleared its throat again, harder this time, as if unimpressed by the countdown.

Fainrose, meanwhile, had turned back to the anchor traces, and Lolly, who had learned over the past day to read her shoulders the way sailors read weather, saw that she had stopped being angry, which meant she had started being useful.

"If we cannot restore it," she asked, "what can we do."

"Something better," Fainrose declared. "Something the vault people never understood, because they were thinking about storage instead of structure." She pulled up the correlation lattice again: the great trembling web of district nodes, domestic nodes, routing lines, all of it still convergent on the black disk marked 4C: the same disk now shedding heat below them. "What do you think a map is for, Lolly? Look again. Not as a map. As a code."

She looked. Habit told her to look away, the way she always had, and for once she did not.

"There's redundancy," she noted slowly. "The same information appears in more than one place. Mrs Chain's sister isn't only in Mrs Chain's house. She's in the ownership record, and the queue token, and the memorial photograph, and the route to the shops, and the two contradictory birth entries---" "Go on." "---and no single one of those is sufficient. But together they overdetermine her." She felt the shape of it arrive. "You don't need the copy. The information isn't in any one place. It's in the pattern across all of them."

Fainrose permitted herself, at last, the full smile. It was brief, and slightly frightening, and Lolly understood that she had just passed an examination she had not known she was sitting.

"That," she explained, "is quantum error correction. How one protects a delicate thing without ever holding it. You do not keep a copy. You spread it out so thin that no local damage can find enough of it to destroy. Any single node may be lost, corrupted, misfiled, or run over by a lorry, and the whole may still be recovered from what remains: provided you know which correlations to trust, and provided you repair them in the right order."

A number at the edge of the display crept towards zero. Nobody looked at it. Looking would not have helped.

"And do we know the right order," Jago asked.\
"No."\
"Ah."\
"But we know it exists," Fainrose replied, "which is more than the Ministry has known about anything since Tuesday."

Beatrix finished typing and stood back. On the main display, at 00:00:41, the words

> RESTORATION ASSET: SEALED PENDING REVIEW

appeared, followed almost immediately by three priority queries from three departments --- one from an office calling itself Retrospective Assurance, already asking whom to blame, all of which Beatrix ignored with the serenity of a woman who had already decided which hill this was.

"How long to work out the order," she asked.

Fainrose was already assembling something on the second console, a syndrome table, Lolly realised, watching the columns fill: not a list of what was broken, but a list of which combinations of breakages could be told apart from one another. "That depends," she replied, "on whether we can measure the damage without measuring the thing."

Lolly stared at her. "Is that possible."

"It is the only thing that is possible," Fainrose stated. "You do not ask the patient how they are. You ask whether their left hand and right hand still agree. That is a syndrome measurement. The answer tells you where the injury is and nothing whatever about who they are, which is precisely why it does not hurt them."

She glanced up. "It is the one form of inspection this Ministry never learned, because it requires wanting to know something other than an answer."

Below them, in the anchor housing, the cathedral cleared its throat once more.

Mrs Chain looked at the ceiling, then at the floor, then at Fainrose. "Dr Fainrose."\
"Mrs Chain," Fainrose replied.\
"When you have finished," Mrs Chain asked, "will my sister be my sister."

Fainrose did not soften it, and Lolly thought better of her for it. "She will be recoverable," she stated. "That is not the same as unharmed. But it is the difference between a person and a form, and I intend to spend the rest of the evening defending it."

Mrs Chain's hands were very still on the railing. "Her name," she informed her, "is Margaret. She takes her tea with too much milk and she laughs at the wrong jokes. If you bring me back a file that answers to Margaret, I shall call that mercy. If you bring me back a form that does not know the milk, I shall call that what it is." She nodded once. "Then I shall make the tea, and you will all stop apologising and start working."

Lolly reached for the green notebook.

She wrote:

No copy exists. No copy can exist.

Then, below it:

But it is written everywhere at once, faintly, and that is stronger

than a copy, provided one repairs it in the right order.

And then, after a moment, in smaller letters:

They built a vault. They should have built a code.

Beatrix did not look up from the seal. "I can file a theorem," she noted. "I cannot file a hope."

Rule: There is no perfect copy of an unknown state; what can be saved lives in the pattern, not the vault.

Lesson for this chapter: [No-Cloning and Quantum Error Correction](#lesson-8-no-cloning-theorem)

# **Chapter 9: The Two Columns**

By the next morning, at eight, she had been downstairs. The requisition archive occupied a basement room designed for files and since surrendered to them. Gideon sat at the central table with his coat still on and a stack of ledgers arranged in a kind of apologetic order, because the heating in the annex had an interpretive relationship with winter.

He spoke, even now, in the same soft, unhurried register he used for everything, as though raising his voice were one more form of clumsiness he had long since trained himself out of.

"You look as if you have been here all night," Lolly said.

"I have been here all night," replied Gideon. "The distinction is one I have been meaning to mention to someone." He took the tea she had brought, missed the saucer by the usual margin, and recovered both without spilling, which she chose not to comment on. "Vale signed for the vault isolation. Voller countersigned. The second page of 11-C is in a different folder, in a different year, and nobody who signed the first page appears to have seen it." "That's going to matter," she noted. "Yes. I thought I'd mention it before I walked into anything."

He did not say what he thought she might walk into. She noticed the omission and decided not to ask him to fill it in.

She looked at him. He looked at the ledger. The heating made a decision and reversed it.

"You're doing the useful part," she remarked.

"I'm reading other people's handwriting. That's not the useful part. That's the part I can do without breaking the building." He turned a page. "Go upstairs. Something on paper is coming. Paper is never a social call."

She went. At six minutes past nine, he was proved right.

The order came down on paper, which was itself a bad sign.

Ministries that intend to be reasonable send email. Email can be amended, forwarded, or quietly forgotten. Paper is sent when someone senior wishes there to be an object later.

Beatrix Sloan read it twice at the annex reception desk, then carried it to the long table in the observation room and put it down in front of Stephanie Fainrose without comment.

Fainrose read it. Then she laughed: one short, unlovely sound, like a chair leg on tile.

"Oh, they've done it," she managed. "They've actually legislated it."

Lolly Wren leaned over. The document ran to a page and a half and had passed through at least four hands, each of which had engineered it worse. She thought of the ledger downstairs (a second page in the wrong folder, in the wrong year), and said nothing. Not yet.

> DIRECTIVE 11-C: CIVIC RECOVERY ASSURANCE

In order that public confidence be maintained during the current period of adjustment, all recovery operations shall be conducted so as to satisfy both of the following conditions simultaneously:

1\. The precise present configuration of each affected dwelling, record, and person shall be established and certified to district level, such that no citizen enquiry may receive an indeterminate response.

2\. The precise rate and direction of ongoing change in each affected dwelling, record, and person shall be established and certified to district level, such that all future states may be projected and no citizen enquiry may receive an unforecastable response.

Recovery may not proceed until both certificates are held.

Signed, on behalf of the Board, C. Vale

Lolly read it again. "Both," she echoed.

"Both," Fainrose replied. "To arbitrary precision. Simultaneously. With certificates."\
"That's---"\
"Yes."\
"That's not a difficult instruction."\
"No," considered Fainrose, "a difficult instruction is one you fail. This is an instruction you cannot begin." She set the paper down very precisely, squaring it with the edge of the table, which Lolly had learned was what she did instead of throwing things. "Somebody has looked at physics, disliked the terms, and issued a rebuttal."

Jago Flint, who had spent the night in a chair and looked entirely unaffected by it in a way Lolly found personally offensive, ventured, "Is it truly impossible, or merely fiendishly hard? I ask because I have made a living in the distinction."

"There is no distinction."\
"There is always a distinction."\
"Not here." Fainrose pulled a blackboard on castors round from the wall. Something in the room's temperature changed; even Voller, hovering by the door, stopped pretending to consult his clipboard. "Mr Flint. If I insist on knowing exactly where a thing is, what have I done?"

Jago considered. "Pinned it."

"Yes. And if I ask, with equal insistence, exactly where it is going?"\
"You have asked for a different sort of answer."\
"One which the first answer has made worse." She put down the chalk. "They are not two independent facts that a sufficiently determined civil servant can requisition. The more tightly one is fixed, the less honestly the other can be certified. There is no apparatus, budget, or signature that alters the bargain."

Mrs Elspeth Chain, who had appointed herself to the tea and to the moral summary, poured without being asked --- correctly Earl Grey, unmilked, because that was one fight she had already had and won --- and remarked, "And they have ordered both." "To district level." "With certificates."

It was, everyone had quietly agreed without ever scheduling a meeting about it, a Friday, which meant Jago had already telephoned the order through to the Bengal Lancer on the corner --- the one entirely reliable comfort available anywhere in the Annex, and probably the reason morale had not yet collapsed. Nobody had ever proposed improving the Ministry's own catering. Nobody saw the point of reforming something that worked.

Mrs Chain set down the pot. "Then they have ordered the tide."

"Considerably worse," corrected Fainrose. "The tide would refuse and continue being the tide. A civil service does not refuse. It complies."

Lolly felt the floor of the argument give slightly beneath her. "What does compliance look like," she asked, "when the thing is impossible?"

Fainrose looked at her for a long moment. "That," she replied, "is the only question in the directive."

The answer arrived at ten forty in the form of a young woman from Certification named Miss Adaeze Pike, who had a laptop, a lanyard, and the specific brightness of a person doing well within a system she had not yet examined. She was neat in a way that looked effortful rather than natural, hair pinned back with the kind of precision that suggested practice, and the particular posture of someone who had been told she had a bright future and had decided to keep proving it true.

She was, Lolly thought, entirely blameless, and she disliked what was about to happen to her.

"We've had 11-C too," she offered cheerfully, setting up at the end of the table. "Field Certification. We've been running since seven."

Beatrix's head came up. "Running what?"\
"Both columns." Miss Pike turned the laptop so that they could see. "Column A is present configuration. Column B is rate of change. Every affected address gets a line."

Miss Pike's spreadsheet, Lolly saw, had the moral structure of a man who asks you for your height and your speed of growth at the same instant and then fines you because the two answers refuse to both be exact.

The spreadsheet was beautiful. Lolly had spent long enough in electronics to recognise a well-built sheet: conditional formatting, locked headers, validation on every field. Somebody had cared about this, and she felt, for one traitorous moment, the old satisfaction of a well-soldered joint: a thing built so it would not fail you, which she had not let herself miss in years.

Every single row was filled.

Lolly's stomach did a small, professional flip, the one she associated with a circuit that worked perfectly and would therefore be much harder to fix later.

"How," said Fainrose.\
Miss Pike blinked. "How what, sorry?"\
"How did you fill Column B?"

"Oh, from the survey," Miss Pike explained. "The Continuity Review data. We take the certified present configuration, compare it to the previous certified present configuration, divide by the interval. That gives rate of change." She smiled, pleased with the tidiness of it. "It's rather elegant, actually. The same measurement serves both columns."

There was a pause of perhaps three seconds. Then Fainrose asked, very gently, "Miss Pike, would you sit down?"

Miss Pike sat down, because that was the sort of voice it had been.

"When you certified the present configuration," Fainrose asked, "what did the surveyor do to the dwelling?"

"Scanned it. Object by object. It's a full-resolution pass, so every element is fixed to certified state. That's what makes Column A reliable."

"Fixed."\
"Yes." "And then you scanned it again a week later, compared the results, divided the difference by seven days, and called the result the rate of change of the house."\
"Exactly."

"Miss Pike," Fainrose informed her, "that is not a measurement of the rate of change of the house. It is a measurement of the rate at which your own instrument disturbed it."

Miss Pike's expression did not change immediately. Then it did.

"No," she protested. "No, because the disturbance is calibrated out. There's a correction factor. Annex Four."\
"Show me Annex Four."

She showed her. Fainrose read it in about eleven seconds, and Lolly, who had developed a reasonable ear for her silences, understood that the eleven seconds were courtesy.

"This factor," Fainrose continued, "was derived by comparing scan results against a reference configuration."\
"Yes."\
"Held in the cold vault."\
Miss Pike's fingers stopped on the trackpad. "The vault's been sealed since last night," she said. "There's a note. I assumed it was procedural."\
"It was me," said Beatrix.\
"Why?" "Because it is a photograph of a conversation," Beatrix replied, "and I did not intend to let anyone restore the country from it."

Miss Pike rallied, because she was, Lolly thought, exactly the kind of person who rallied. "Then we recalibrate properly. A finer scan. A gentler instrument: less disturbance, a smaller error---"

"You may build the gentlest instrument the Ministry has ever funded," stated Fainrose, "and you will still not hold both numbers to arbitrary precision at once. This is not a defect in your equipment, Miss Pike. It is a fact about the house." She reached for the chalk out of habit, and did not use it. "Physics has a name for it. [The Uncertainty Principle](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=dgoA_jmGIcA). Known since 1927. The Ministry is the first institution confident enough to overrule it by memorandum."

"Then Column B is---" She stopped and started again, and got it out properly the second time, which she respected. "Then Column B is the instrument looking at itself."

"Yes," Fainrose confirmed. "Nine thousand rows."\
"Yes." "And Column A is real."\
"Column A is an answer," Fainrose continued. "It is real in the way a verdict is real. It became true when you asked."

Miss Pike put both hands flat on the table. "So the more precisely we certified where things were," she considered slowly, "the more nonsense we generated about where they were going."

"Yes."\
"And the directive requires both."\
"Yes."\
"So the harder we comply, the worse it gets."\
"Now," said Fainrose, "you have understood 11-C better than the man who signed it."

It was Mrs Chain who asked the question that mattered, because it was always Mrs Chain who asked the question that mattered. "What has it done to the people?"

Nobody answered.

"Dr Fainrose. Nine thousand rows. They are addresses. Somebody lives at each of them." She folded her hands. "What has it done to them?"

Fainrose crossed to the main display, pulled up the domestic event stream, and filtered it. Lolly saw her hesitate before she let it render: the first time she had seen her flinch from her own data.

The pattern was unmistakable once you knew to look for it.

Every dwelling certified to the highest precision in Column A showed, in the days following, the wildest instability in every other respect. Routes to the shops changed on alternate mornings. Correspondence arrived addressed to residents who were and were not the residents. Two households' front doors had, for eleven hours, both opened onto the same hallway, an incident logged as shared vestibule, low priority. An ambulance sent to a certified address in Sub-District 2 arrived to find the certified door, at the certified number, opening onto a stairwell the certification did not yet know had been demolished. A man in Sub-District 9 had been certified so exactly that his own employment record could no longer predict where he would be at nine in the morning, and had accordingly been marked unreliable.

Jago studied the list without visible alarm. "I have known men in my own trade less reliably located than that," he remarked, "and none of them needed a memorandum to explain it."

"They pinned them," Lolly breathed.

"They pinned them," repeated Fainrose, "and then wrote down that they were erratic. The more certain the pin, the more erratic the record; the more erratic the record, the stronger the case for a further pin." She turned. "It is a machine for manufacturing exactly the instability it was built to remove, and it will run as long as it is funded, because every cycle produces evidence that it is needed."

Lolly had watched the Ministry force a false certainty before: pin a cat, pin a well, pin a number that did not want to sit still. This was not that. Nobody was forcing one wrong answer; they were demanding two right ones at once, and physics had simply declined to hold both. That struck her as worse: not a mistake to correct, but a demand that could never be satisfied, however hard anyone tried.

Jago exhaled. "That is the most administratively perfect thing I have ever heard."

"It is not a flaw in the Initiative," stated Fainrose. "It is the Initiative. Vale does not need reality to be simple. He needs it to be reportably simple. Those are different projects. Only one is achievable, and it is achieved by insisting on two certificates that cannot both be honest."

Beatrix had gone very quiet, which in her case meant she had stopped being furious and started constructing.

"Miss Pike," she noted. Miss Pike glanced up. "Column B is fabricated."\
"Not deliberately---"\
"I did not say fraudulently. I said fabricated. Do you dispute it?"

A long pause. Then, to her lasting credit: "No. I don't."\
"Will you say so in writing?"

Miss Pike looked at the spreadsheet --- nine thousand rows, locked headers, conditional formatting, four weeks of good work in service of an impossibility, and Lolly watched her decide. Whatever else came of this appalling week, she thought, that decision had been worth witnessing.

"Yes," she managed.

Beatrix did not smile when she said it. Winning, this week, had stopped looking like something worth smiling about.

"Then we have him," Beatrix declared. "Not for the black hole. Not for the houses. Nobody is ever hanged for a black hole." She picked up the directive and held it out. "For requiring, in writing, over signature, a certification that cannot exist, and then acting on it. That is not a scientific dispute. That is a false instrument in a live system."

Fainrose looked at the paper with something like respect.

"He wrote it down," she noted.\
"He wrote it down," Beatrix confirmed.

Mrs Chain refilled the pot.

"Men like that always do," she said. "They cannot bear a thing to be true unless it has been copied twice and misfiled once."

Lolly opened the green notebook marked

> PRIVATE/UNHELPFUL

She wrote:

You may know where it is. You may know where it is going. You may not have both, and the shortfall is not a shortage of effort.

Then, below:

They demanded both. So the second column had to be invented.

And then they punished people for failing to match the invention.

And at the bottom, smaller:

The cruelty is not the uncertainty. The cruelty is the certificate.

The two columns were meant to be reassuring. One recorded the state the Ministry had certified. The other recorded the state it intended to certify next. Lolly stared at them until she understood the trap: the system had no column for *we don't know*. It had only two kinds of certainty, and both were now being fed back into the thing they were supposed to describe.

"How long?" she asked.\
Fainrose glanced at the clock. "Until it becomes self-consistent."\
"And if it does?"

She did not answer, and Lolly had learned enough this week not to ask a third time.

She closed the notebook. She could not know, any more than Fainrose could, what the Ministry would do once its own numbers finally agreed with each other --- and some part of her understood, with the flat, unsentimental clarity of a joint that has either taken or not, that nobody was going to hand her that certainty in time for it to be any use. She could know exactly where the trouble presently sat, or she could go and do something about where it was heading. Not both, she thought, and did not let herself find that funny. She picked up her coat.

"Where are you going?" Jago asked.

"Downstairs," Lolly replied. "Gideon found something odd about the second page of 11-C this morning, and I don't think anyone's asked him about it since."

Rule: Quantum mechanics permits a precise location or a precise motion, but not two limitless certificates at once.

Lesson for this chapter: [Uncertainty: Position and Momentum](#lesson-9-uncertainty-principle)

# **Chapter 10: The Moon That Was Pending**

The complaint came in from Outer Fenwick at half past seven in the evening, which was itself unusual, because Outer Fenwick did not officially exist until the moon came up, and the moon did not officially come up until enough people had filled in the form saying they had seen it.

Lolly Wren read the complaint three times before she accepted that she had read it correctly.

She checked the letterhead twice, in case someone in Filing had finally developed a sense of humour. Filing had never shown any previous sign of one, but there was a first time for everything, and she found herself hoping this was it.

"Ratepayer states," she read aloud, "that on the night of the fourteenth, insufficient observation returns were logged by nine p.m., and the moon"---she checked the wording again---"'did not come up properly, and hung about at the edge looking unconvincing until gone eleven, at which point enough of the residents had done their forms and it committed.'"

Beatrix Sloan did not look up from her boxes. "Committed to what?"\
"Being a moon, I think," Lolly said.\
"That is not how moons work, Ms Wren."\
"I am aware of that, Ms Sloan. I am reading you the complaint."

Stephanie Fainrose, who had not slept in a describable way for two days and had reached the particular plateau of tiredness at which she became lucid about everything except her own coat, which she had put on inside out, took the sheet from her.

She read it once. She did not read it a second time, which Lolly had learned to find ominous.

"Outer Fenwick," she decided. "Get the car."

The road out to Outer Fenwick ran along the ridge above Sub-District 6. At a quarter to nine, with the sky doing something tentative and unfinished in the east, Lolly first understood the shape of what they were driving towards. People sat on their front steps. Not many (eleven, perhaps fourteen, scattered along half a mile of terraced housing), each with a folding chair, a flask, and a clipboard, all facing the same direction with the settled patience of racegoers told that the principal attraction was running late.

Somewhere down the terrace a radio was playing through an open window, turned low, and a dog barked twice at nothing in particular before giving it up as a bad job; nobody on the steps so much as glanced round, the way people stop reacting to a sound once they've learned it doesn't mean the moon.

Whatever happened on the fourteenth was not closed, Lolly understood with a small lurch. It was also tonight's rota, and nobody on that pavement looked confident about it.

The far side of the ridge fell away towards Sub-District 6, which lay under a continuity freeze, Vale's order, to hold the local grid steady while 4C was argued about elsewhere. It showed from up here as a grid of windows all lit the same flat yellow, not one of them changing. The people on the steps had their backs to it.

"What are they doing?" Lolly asked.\
"Observing," Fainrose replied. "Or preparing to. Look at the forms."

She looked. Each clipboard held a single sheet headed

> OUTER FENWICK NOCTURNAL AMENITY:
>
> CONFIRMATION OF SIGHTING

with boxes for name, address, and time, a tick beside the words *I confirm that I have observed the lunar body described in Schedule 4 and consider it to be present*, and, in small print, a note that disputes over classical adequacy should be cross-referred to Form 4C.

"Schedule 4," Lolly noted.\
"Don't," Fainrose warned, in the tone of a woman reserving strength for worse news later.

Jago Flint, driving, took the last bend onto the ridge somewhat faster than the road recommended and remarked, without turning round, "I've heard of this. Didn't credit it. There's a district over Coldharrow way that does the same with its high-street lamp, the one by the butcher's. Goes out on cloudy nights if nobody's looked at it by six. Everyone assumed the wiring was at fault."

"It is not a fault in the wiring," Fainrose corrected.

They parked outside a house with a hand-lettered sign in the window

> FENWICK RESIDENTS' MOON WATCH,
>
> EST. FOURTEEN MONTHS AGO

and a woman of about sixty came out to meet them before they had properly got out of the car. She wiped her hands on an apron which suggested that she had left something on a stove from sheer bureaucratic duty. She was broad and weathered in the way of someone who had spent most of a life outdoors without ever quite meaning to, with the sort of grey, no-nonsense perm that had outlasted several fashions on the strength of never having asked their opinion.

Up close she smelled of coal smoke and simmering stew, with the specific damp-wool note of someone who had come outside without a coat for the ninth night running and stopped noticing the cold somewhere around night three.

"You'll be from the Ministry," she managed, without much enthusiasm. "I'm Mrs Prosser. I do the rota."\
"The rota for what, precisely?" Fainrose asked.

"The moon." Mrs Prosser ventured it as one might say the bins: a fact of civic life requiring management rather than wonder. "It came in with the Continuity Review, love. Fourteen months back. Somebody at the Ministry decided Outer Fenwick's night sky was an 'unresolved amenity pending classical adequacy determination' (those are the exact words; I kept the letter) and until it was resolved, resolving it required sufficient corroborated observation from the resident population. So. Rota."

Lolly had the green notebook marked

> PRIVATE/UNHELPFUL

out already. "How many observers constitute sufficient?"

"Forty-one," replied Mrs Prosser, with the flat authority of a woman who had fought this battle with a spreadsheet and won. "It was thirty-eight for the first six months. Then they moved the district boundary, picked up three more houses, and never revised the number back down. I have written to them nine times about it, and I would like it on record that I have written to them nine times."

Forty-one signatures to keep a moon. Lolly wrote the number down, then wrote "photons" underneath it, then drew a line through the first number with more satisfaction than a civil servant is strictly allowed.

"And on the night it dips below forty-one?"

"It gets vague," Mrs Prosser explained. "Not gone. Vague. You get a sort of grey smudge where it ought to be, like a photograph somebody's thumb was over half of. Cats won't sit by the window, and my Reginald says his knee plays up, though Reginald's knee plays up regardless and I don't put that one on the Ministry. I have standards. The lamp on Cutler's Row goes soft the same nights, if you want to know."

Somewhere behind the house a voice called out a number --- thirty-six, it sounded like --- and Mrs Prosser did not so much as glance towards it, which told Lolly more about how often this happened than any of the forms had.

Fainrose had gone very still, in the specific way Lolly now recognised as her thinking so hard that stillness was the only way to contain it.

"Mrs Prosser," she instructed. "On the night the complaint describes, talk me through it minute by minute."

Mrs Prosser was, it turned out, exactly the witness a desperate physicist could have ordered from a catalogue. She had logged everything. At 8:52 there had been thirty-four confirmed observation returns: people on steps, forms ticked, the usual quorum falling short by the usual margin, which normally resolved itself by nine as the last few latecomers emerged with tea.

But on the fourteenth, seven regular observers had been at the church hall for the whist drive, and the moon (Mrs Prosser used the word with the weary precision of someone who had stopped finding it strange) had "sort of dithered." It was visible from some houses and not from others. One household reported a crescent; the house two doors along, looking at the identical patch of sky, reported nothing at all, "which caused words, because the Hendersons have form for exaggerating and everyone said so to their faces."

"And then," remarked Mrs Prosser, "gone eleven, the whist drive let out, and everybody came home and had cocoa and looked up out of habit more than hope, and there it was. Properly there. Committed, like the letter said."

"Like the complaint said," Lolly corrected gently.\
"Same thing round here," agreed Mrs Prosser.

Fainrose walked to the pavement's edge and tipped her head back. It was presently offering a perfectly ordinary, extremely convincing gibbous moon, hanging in high cloud with the smug indifference of an object that had never once been asked to complete a declaration about itself.

"It is not that observation makes it real," she explained, half to herself and half to Lolly. She had learned that this half-address was the closest thing to a lecture she was going to get, and had trained herself to write quickly. "That is the vulgar version, and it is wrong, and it is exactly the version this Ministry has legislated into a rota."

"Then what is happening?"

"Does that thing flicker on an ordinary night, curtains shut, nobody on this ridge at all?"\
"No."\
"Why not, if nobody's confirmed it?"\
"Because something else already has," offered Lolly, working it out as she said it.

"Everything else has. Sunlight leaves it in every direction physics allows, every second (each photon its own small arrow, spinning towards a windowpane, a puddle, a bored pigeon) and by the time enough of them land, [only one moon is left standing in the record](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=igsuIuI_HAQ). That's [decoherence](#glDecoherence), and it runs faster than forty-one clipboards ever could. For forty minutes, that moon did what nothing moon-sized should manage for longer than an instant: it sat in [superposition](#glSuperposition), neither up nor not-up, because something was stopping it settle."

"Then why did it dither?"

"Because someone built a filter." Now she turned to look at Lolly, and there was something close to fury in it, banked low. "Somewhere in that paperwork is a device (probably a receiver mast, modest and municipal) coupled to the observation quorum. It doesn't touch the moon. It touches the town: damping the background contact that would decohere Outer Fenwick's sky, releasing it again only once the forms say the humans have earned a definite one. Out of every shape a superposition can fall into, decoherence leaves only the dull, stable ones standing ([einselection](#glEinselection)) and a moon that just hangs there being a moon is a pointer st[ate: an answer](#glPointer)that survives being asked the same question by everything, constantly. This Ministry built a machine to interrupt that, so forty-one signatures could be sold back as the cure."

Lolly thought of the number forty-one, and then, without quite knowing why yet, of the windows on the far side of the ridge, all lit the same flat yellow, and did not like the shape the thought was taking.

Lolly looked from her to Mrs Prosser to the moon and felt the particular vertigo she had come, over the past few days, to recognise as understanding arriving slightly ahead of comfort.

"They're not making it real by looking," she considered slowly. "They've broken the thing that lets it be real without looking, and charged the residents to fix it. With a rota."

"With a rota," Fainrose agreed, "and quorum thresholds, and presumably a line item."

"Why?"\
"Because a moon requiring forty-one confirmed observations to commit is a moon the district can be billed for," considered Beatrix Sloan, who had got out of the car at some point and already found, unerringly, the one sentence in the whole business that was actually about money. "Ongoing amenity-resolution services. I should like to see the invoice."

Mrs Prosser brightened. "There is an invoice," she announced, and fetched a folder kept for exactly this moment.

Beatrix read it from the bottom up, the way she read everything, and went quiet in a way that had nothing to do with the cold. "Requisition code," she noted. "Continuity Directorate, Sub-District allocation, authorising officer C. Vale." She raised her head. "Same office holding Sub-District Six under a freeze, twenty minutes down that road."

"Coincidence," said Jago, without much conviction, "isn't a word I've heard used correctly this decade."

Fainrose gazed down at the frozen grid of yellow windows, none of them changing, and did not seem reassured. "One receiver mast for forty-one houses and a stubborn moon," she noted, quiet now, "and a sub-district under the same freeze, at a scale that would take rather more than forty-one clipboards if it ever slipped." She did not finish the thought aloud. Lolly wrote it down anyway.

Nobody said much on the drive back. Fainrose kept the invoice on her knee and a pen that did not stop moving, which Lolly had learned meant a letter was already being drafted that the Ministry would not enjoy. She could not undo Sub-District 6's freeze from the back seat of a car at midnight, and she knew it. She kept writing anyway.

Once, Lolly thought she saw a light out on the ridge blink off and come back, too fast and too even for an ordinary bulb. She didn't mention it. She had the distinct impression nobody else in the car was going to mention it either.

Lolly took out the green notebook and wrote:

Nobody has to look at the moon for it to stay a moon. The air is looking at it constantly, on nobody's authority, and that is a mercy, not a technicality.

Then, underneath:

They found a machine that quietened the mercy, called the silence a shortfall, and billed the street to fix what they had broken.

And at the bottom, in the smaller hand she used for the sentences she did not entirely want to admit to writing:

The moon does not need forty-one witnesses. It needs the sky to be allowed to keep noticing it. Most things do.

Rule: The moon stays definite because the whole sky keeps physically recording it, not because forty-one signatures have been collected.

Lesson for this chapter: [Decoherence and Einselection](#lesson-10-decoherence-and-einselection)

# **Chapter 11: An Uninvited Reflection**

The Phase Two papers arrived at two, in eleven boxes, and Beatrix Sloan had read four of them by half past five with the expression of a woman conducting a post-mortem on someone she had known.

The covering memorandum gave a start date. Monday week. Nine days.

Beatrix had underlined \*nine\* twice, then stopped herself, as though underlining a number twice might be a way of making it listen.

Lolly Wren had been given box seven, which held the consent architecture, and it was in box seven that she found the thing that made her go and stand outside for a while.

"It's the invitation," she announced when she came back in.\
Fainrose looked up. "Say that again."

"Phase Two doesn't take memories." She put the folder on the table. "It can't. Not directly. A memory cannot be read out by external survey. It only becomes definite when the person is asked."

Beatrix repeated, "So they ask."

"So they ask," Lolly confirmed. "And the asking is the whole mechanism. If a citizen fills in a form, describes the memory, signs the declaration, then the memory becomes whatever the form says, because the form was the measurement." She turned the folder round. "Look at the title of the volume."

Fainrose read it aloud. "Voluntary Recollection Assurance: Establishing Lawful Access Through Citizen-Initiated Enquiry."\
"It cannot come in," mentioned Lolly, "unless you invite it."

Jago Flint, who had been asleep in the chair again and had apparently been listening through it, observed without opening his eyes, "Oh, that's an old shape."

"What is?" Lolly asked.\
"That," he replied, sitting up. "Cannot cross the threshold uninvited. Very old. Doesn't usually turn up in policy documents."

At that point the lamps dimmed by one degree, in a manner Lolly had last seen in Mrs Chain's hallway and had learned to distrust profoundly.

"Ah," said a voice from the doorway. "Forgive me. I did knock, but the door was in two minds."

The voice was low, dry as old paper, and carried the ghost of an accent too old to belong to any country still drawing its own borders.

The man standing there was extremely old and radiantly untidy.

He had a great disorganised nimbus of white hair, a moustache of magnificent indifference, a cardigan that had outlived several arguments, and no shoes. His face was one everyone in the room had seen on posters and school laboratory walls. The resemblance produced not recognition but a sort of vertigo.

He smelled, faintly, of spent matches and photographic developer, as though he had stepped down off one of those laboratory-wall posters himself.

He beamed at them. "Albrecht Eilstein," he announced. "Please don't."\
"Don't what?" Lolly asked.\
"Say it. Everybody says it. I have had eleven decades of it, and it was tiresome by 1931."

He came in without hurrying, and Lolly noticed that the doorframe appeared to accommodate him rather than the reverse. "No relation," he added. "Well --- not in the sense you mean by relation."

He said it the way a man corrects a spelling on a form, without heat. "Born human, if it helps. Ulm, the fourteenth of March, 1879. The rest came later, and a good deal more slowly than people assume."

Fainrose had risen from her chair.

"Dr Fainrose," remarked Eilstein, warmly. "I have read you. The correlation paper. Beautiful. Wrong in one place, which we shall come to, but beautiful."

"Where wrong?"\
"Later. You will enjoy it more when you are frightened."

He surveyed round the room with enormous, benevolent interest. "Ms Sloan. Mr Flint. Mrs Chain. Ms Wren." He stopped. "Ms Wren, you have found the invitation clause."

"About ten minutes ago."\
"Yes," remarked Eilstein. "That is when I could come."

Nobody moved.

Mrs Elspeth Chain, who had developed a settled policy of addressing the impossible directly and letting it explain itself, demanded, "Who are you?"

Eilstein considered the question with visible relish, like a man choosing from a good cellar.

"I am the oldest consequence," he replied. "There is no better phrase. Some have tried. The Babylonians had one, but it does not translate."

Beatrix objected, "That is not an answer."\
"No. Try this," Eilstein said.

He crossed to the long window. The late sun came through it and lay on the floor in a rhombus. He stepped into the middle of the rhombus and stood there.

He cast no shadow.

Lolly peered at the floor. The chairs had shadows. The table had a shadow. The old man in the cardigan stood in full August light, and the floor beneath him was as bright as the floor beside him.

"There," reflected Eilstein comfortably. "That usually does it."\
Jago noted, "You'll want the mirror as well."\
"You are a quick young man, and I shall try to keep you alive," Eilstein approved.

He turned to the glass-fronted cabinet on the far wall, in which every one of them was reflected: the sunlight, the doorway, and the eleven boxes.

He was not.

Mrs Chain took a firm hold of the back of a chair.

"You are a vampire," she stated.

Jago, unbothered, considered this like a man revisiting an old argument. "I asked him that once, in a stairwell in '19," he mentioned. "He told me he only drinks Bloody Marys because he likes the vodka, and blames the pallor on the fluorescent tubes in Requisitions. I did not believe a word of it then either."

"That is the local word and I have stopped resisting it," Eilstein agreed. "It is roughly as accurate as calling the sun a lamp. But yes, approximately, functionally, for our purposes this afternoon."

"Then what do you drink?"

Eilstein's beam faded, not into menace, but into something worse, which was seriousness.

"Ah," he approved. "You go straight to it. Good. I do so dislike the ones who ask about bats."

He came away from the window and sat, uninvited, in the chair Jago had vacated.

"I do not drink blood, Mrs Chain. Blood is a courier." He laced his old hands together. "When a question is put to the world, and the world is obliged to produce one answer, the other answers are not filed. They cease, and in ceasing they release something." He shrugged. "I take that. I have taken it since the first measurement."

Nobody in the room breathed for a count of three. Even Jago, who made a habit of not being impressed by anything, had gone very still.

Lolly felt the room tilt. "You feed on collapse."\
"On the discarded branch, yes. The road not taken, though your poet was sentimental, and I am a diner."\
Fainrose ventured, carefully, "The interpretation with the branches. Everett's."

Lolly thought, with a lurch, of a pencilled note in her own hand that had crossed itself out before she had finished writing it, in her first hour on this case. She had filed it as a glitch. She was no longer sure the word applied.

"Your people insist on calling it the [Many-Worlds interpretation](#glMany)," replied Eilstein, with distaste. "I did not care for it before I died, and dying has not improved my opinion. I merely eat what it leaves on the floor."

"Then---" Lolly's voice came out wrong, and she started again. "Then the Simplification Initiative---"\
"Is the finest thing that has happened to me since agriculture," replied Albrecht Eilstein.

The silence that followed had a physical weight.

"Nine thousand dwellings," he continued, "each forced from a rich state into a thin one, tens of thousands of alternatives extinguished per address, per week, with certificates. It is not a meal. A meal is small. This is industry. A national programme with a budget line. There will, I should imagine, be a procurement code."

He said it the way another man might complain about a parking permit, and that, more than the shadow or the mirror, was the detail Lolly found she could not put back down.

Lolly opened the green notebook and, before Eilstein could continue, wrote quickly in the margin:

*I hid in equations for ten years. Then in Transit Reconciliation for five.*

*Now in notebooks, keeping score of things without having to act.*

*When did noticing become another way not to decide?*

She closed the book. Looked up.

Eilstein was watching her with an expression she had no word for: not curiosity, not kindness, though it wore kindness's clothes.

"You have just asked yourself an old, old question, Ms Wren," he noted. "I will not answer it for you. Nobody outside can: that's rather the point." Something in his face went almost gentle. "But this much: noticing is not nothing. It is only nothing if you stop there."

Fainrose ventured, carefully, "And the anchor. 4C."

Eilstein's expression changed for the first time.

"No," he stated. "That is not mine, and I want it understood. I did not ask them to build a sink or hang a county from an evaporating hole. That is not appetite, Dr Fainrose; that is waste, and I have never had patience for waste."

He sat forward. "This is why I have come. Not to gloat."\
"Then why?" asked Fainrose.

"Because Phase Two is a mistake even by my standards," considered Eilstein, "and because I appear to be, in this room, the only person old enough to be genuinely conservative about reality."

He picked up box seven's folder and turned it over with distaste. "Ms Sloan. You audit for a living. When your ledger disagrees with itself, which entry do you trust?"

"Neither," Beatrix replied. "I trace to source."

"Just so." He tapped the folder's cover. "Houses I can dine on for a century. But memory is the record of which branch was taken: how the universe keeps its accounts. Collapse it by questionnaire, and you falsify the ledger. Then nobody can tell what happened: including me, including the system trying to repair itself."

Lolly observed slowly, "The error correction."

"Yes," replied Eilstein, real approval warming his voice, as though he had been waiting for exactly that question. "Your Dr Fainrose is right that the country can be recovered from its correlations. But ask leading questions about what people remember, and every answer becomes true the moment it is given: the agreements will be manufactured, the syndrome measurement will come back clean, and the code will report itself intact. Around nothing."

Beatrix noted, "It would look like a successful recovery."

"It would be a successful recovery of the wrong state. Certified. Permanently. With no residue for anyone to appeal to, and none for anyone to eat." He sat back. "You have made me pedagogical. I resent it."

Mrs Chain, who had not sat down, said, "You want us to stop them."\
"I want the ledger to survive. Yes."\
"For your own reasons."

"Entirely for my own reasons," Eilstein agreed. "Mrs Chain, I have never once acted from virtue. But my reasons and yours are pointing the same way this month."

He glanced at the boxes. "Also, I am the only one of you who has read Phase Two, invited into it this morning by a Deputy Under-Secretary who filled in a form about his mother."

Fainrose reminded him, "You told me my paper was wrong in one place."\
"I did."\
"Where?"

Albrecht Eilstein smiled. It was not a comfortable smile, and Lolly understood that they had arrived at the thing the old man had actually come to say.

"You are looking at [the measurement problem](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=BU8Lg_R2DL0)," he noted, "wearing a cardigan. Enjoy the novelty. It will not last."

"You write," Eilstein continued, "as though a measurement were an event: a thing that happens at a time, after which there is one answer."

He shook his head. "That is the working assumption of every laboratory on this planet. It is not true. When your Mr Venn scanned Mrs Chain's mantelpiece, what became definite for Mr Venn did not become definite for the mantelpiece, or the county, or me. There is no single moment at which the universe decides: only a widening region of agreement, spreading at the speed of gossip."

Lolly felt the hair on her arms rise.

"Then when Vale froze Sub-District 6---"

"He froze it for the grid," Eilstein explained. "Not for the woman remembering her sister. She was inside. Different question. Different answer."

Fainrose went very still. "That's not new," she considered slowly. "That's [Wigner's friend](#glWigners), dressed in a filing cabinet."\
Eilstein inclined his head, almost approving. "Dressed in a filing cabinet. Yes. I may steal that."\
"Then how does anyone calculate what's real," Lolly asked, "if there's no moment where it happens?"

Eilstein looked pleased to be asked. "Now you are asking the interesting question instead of the frightening one. There is a way. A younger man worked it out, after they buried the version of me you are looking at. He does not ask what happened; he asks what could have happened (every route), gives each one a small turning arrow, and adds them up to see where they point. It works to more decimal places than anything else I have seen tried, and I cannot tell you why. I will not explain it so you understand it: I do not understand it that way myself. I can only tell you it calculates."

For exactly one word his fluency snagged --- gone before anyone could be sure they'd heard it --- as though even he found the next sentence expensive to produce.

Fainrose ventured, quietly, "That's decoherence, isn't it, what you just described. The agreement, spreading."\
"Your word for it," Eilstein returned. "Mine is older, and ruder."

He rose. "There is no view from nowhere, Ms Wren. Your Ministry believes it is standing outside the room. It is not. There is no outside. When it finally asks what everybody remembers, it will not be reading the answer."

He went to the door, paused, and glanced back at the five of them with something that, in a warmer species, might have been affection.

"It will be writing it," finished Albrecht Eilstein. "And it will be inside the sentence when it does. Good evening. Do not invite me anywhere without meaning it."

He left.

The lamps came back up by one degree.

Beatrix glanced up at them as they did, and did not look reassured by getting the light back.

Jago sat down heavily in the chair the old man had used, thought better of it, and chose another. "Not sitting where he sat," he remarked, to nobody in particular. "Old habits."

Beatrix observed, to nobody, "We have an ally who eats the thing we are trying to save."

"He eats the offcuts of the thing we are trying to save," considered Fainrose. "Which is worse, because it means he is telling the truth about wanting the rest intact." She was already turning back to the boxes. "And he is right about the paper."

Mrs Chain finally sat.

"He cast no shadow," she reflected, "and he did not lie to us once. I have met the opposite arrangement all week, and I know which I find more frightening." She folded her hands, unhurried, like a woman settling an account rather than a nerve. "I don't need to like what he is, or trust why he's helping. I need to know what Monday week costs us if we do nothing. So. We work with the vampire."

The green notebook came out again, and Lolly wrote:

> PRIVATE/UNHELPFUL

She wrote:

He cannot enter unless asked. Neither can the measurement. They have built a national programme of asking.

Then:

There is no moment when the universe decides. There is only agreement, spreading. No one is outside the room.

And at the bottom, after a long pause:

Phase Two does not read memory. It writes it, and signs it, and the signature is inside the fact.

Lolly closed the consent form. "If the invitation is the measurement, then every signature is an intervention."\
Beatrix went very still. "How many signatures?"\
Lolly looked at the box. "Nine thousand, if the rollout goes national."

Nobody fashioned a joke. That frightened her more than the vampire had.

"Nine days," Beatrix concluded, "to decide what a memory is allowed to mean."

Rule: If quantum evolution applies to everything, the unanswered question is what makes one answer the one we live with.

Lesson for this chapter: [The Measurement Problem and Wigner's Friend](#lesson-11-wigners-friend)

# **Chapter 12: Standing in the Wave**

Phase Two went live at seven the next morning in four sub-districts --- nine days early, and nowhere near the whole country Beatrix had been warned about --- because Vale had been suspended at midnight and the Board had discovered, on reading his file, that they preferred the version of him that had signed things.

Fainrose had a view, delivered to the syndrome table. "They have taken the one signature off the directive," she said. "Now it has no author, and whoever stands up in that inquiry and says what it did will be thanked for the disruption and not the truth. The cheapest move there is, and they will make it every time."

By eight, Beatrix Sloan had established that the suspension had been proposed by a committee none of whose members would consent to be named, its minutes showing only that the meeting had achieved quorum. By nine she had stopped shouting at telephones and started drafting, which Lolly Wren had learned to recognise as the more dangerous phase. An Inquiry was called for eleven: two hours to make sure it had something to inquire into.

Lolly, by ten in the morning, was no use to anybody.

This was not modesty. She had checked. Fainrose needed silence and stabiliser algebra; Beatrix needed a lawyer; Gideon was in the requisition archive with fifteen years of ledgers. Jago had gone to Croydon on an errand unlikely to be relevant. Mrs Chain had gone to Sub-District 6 to sit with another woman whose sister's name had gone the same way, and had taken the umbrella.

The woman in Sub-District 6 had a classification: P-9, pending status, for a citizen the survey had caught in the middle of something, a name half-said, a journey not yet agreed to, and then required to sign regardless. Beside the box, on file after file, a second field read no action required. The log stood at ninety-one that morning, and was climbing.

By the time Lolly made herself stop checking it, the log had already changed twice more.

Lolly had a green notebook marked PRIVATE/UNHELPFUL containing forty pages of observations, every one of which had turned out, on arrival, to be something a professional had known since 1935.

She went out to sit on the wall by the annex car park, which was where the Duc found her.

She had not heard him arrive, and when she thought about it afterwards she could not say from which direction he had come, or find, in the car park, a car that explained him.

"You are the one who writes things down," murmured a voice.

Lolly looked up.

The man standing over her was slight, elderly, and dressed with an exactness that had gone out of fashion so long ago it had returned as a moral position: a dark suit of beautiful cut, a stiff collar, a narrow grey tie, and gloves. He held a hat. He gave the impression of someone who had been waiting to be noticed, and who considered impatience a failure of breeding.

Something old came with him onto the wall --- beeswax, or cedar, or a wardrobe shut a very long time ago --- the smell, Lolly thought, of a man who had been folded away rather than aged.

"I am sorry to intrude," he began. His English was slow, exact, and unhurried in a way that made Lolly realise how quickly everybody else had been speaking, these last three days. "Louis-Raymond-Victor, Duc de Broccoli, entirely at your service."

Lolly got up. She was not entirely sure why, except that one did.

"Lolly Wren," she said.\
"Yes, Transit Reconciliation," replied de Broccoli, inclining his head a fraction. "I have read your notebook."\
"You have not."\
"Not the object. The shape of it. Dr Fainrose quoted four of your lines to me this morning, to complain about them, which is the highest form of French compliment." He looked at the wall, and lowered himself onto it before she could answer, with the caution of a man testing ice. "It is a long time since I have sat on a wall. I had begun to wonder whether it was still possible."

Lolly sat down beside him.

"You are not with the Ministry," said Lolly.\
"Certainly not," de Broccoli replied.\
Lolly tried again. "Are you with him? The old man. Eilstein."

De Broccoli's face did something complicated and courteous.

"I have known Albrecht Eilstein," he began, "for ninety-one years, and I have never once forgiven him. I would trust him with my life and nothing smaller. He telephoned me last night. He said: Louis, they have a clerk who has understood four things by herself, and she is about to stop." Then De Broccoli turned his head. "Was he correct?"

Lolly looked at the car park.

"I was a physicist," she admitted. "For about eleven minutes. Then electronics for ten years, then Transit Reconciliation. Three days ago I started noticing things in a notebook, and every one turns out to be a standard result with somebody's name on it."

She did not much care how it sounded. "I write that the house is larger than the form and feel clever until somebody names it."

De Broccoli listened with grave attention, as though it were a technical report.

Then he asked, "May I tell you what I was, in 1911?"\
"All right," Lolly replied.

"I was twenty-two. I had taken my degree in history." He said it without emphasis. "My family had been soldiers and diplomats for six hundred years, and I was, by inclination, an archivist. My brother was the physicist. I was the one who read."

"In 1911 my brother served as secretary to a congress of physicists," mentioned de Broccoli, "and brought home the papers. I read them because I read everything." He turned the hat over. "I had no laboratory, no standing. What I had was the habit of a historian: when two authorities disagree, one does not choose. One looks for the document they have both misread."

Lolly asked, "And what were the two authorities?"\
"Light," ventured de Broccoli, "which everybody knew was a wave, and which Einstein had shown behaved in some circumstances as though it were a great number of small hard things."

He shrugged. "The physicists were embarrassed. I was not, because I was not a physicist, and so I took it seriously in both directions at once."

"Meaning?"

"Everyone was asking how a wave could be a particle." He looked at Lolly. "Nobody was asking whether a particle might be a wave. It required no apparatus and no genius: only somebody outside the room, with the wrong training."

"And you were right," Lolly remarked.\
"I was right," de Broccoli agreed, "and it took four years to say it properly. When I noted it, my examiners did not believe it. That is usually how it goes."

Lolly said nothing for a while. "You are telling me the outsider sometimes gets it."

"No." De Broccoli's voice was suddenly quite firm. "That is a comforting story and I dislike it. The outsider usually gets nothing and dies unpublished. I am telling you something narrower." He set the hat down on the wall. "Ms Wren, every observation in your notebook has a name already. Which of them did you write down before you were told the name?"

"All of them. That's rather my point."

"No," said de Broccoli. "That is my point. You are treating the pre-existence of the name as evidence that the observation was worthless. It is evidence that the observation was correct."

He let that sit. "One may receive a piece of physics and possess a fact. Or one may arrive at it and possess the reason, and go on to the next thing, which has no name yet. The second is the only kind that is any use in a crisis, and it is the only kind you have."

Lolly's throat had gone tight. She let it.

It was not only what he had said. It was the register he said it in: patient, exact, allergic to comfort, the same register her mother had used, in the last months, trying to explain why some particular equation would not sit still for her. Her mother had died in a hospital room, Room 137, of a stroke, after --- the family's own phrase, offered for want of a truer one --- thinking too long about quantum mechanics. Lolly had spent the years since quietly checking that description against the medical facts and finding it held up better than a euphemism had any right to.

She had signed the paperwork afterward without being certain of a single line in it, and had promised herself, in the corridor outside that door, never to do that again about anything that mattered. It did not escape her, sitting on a wall with a very old man talking about waves and particles, that the number itself was exactly the kind of coincidence her mother would not have let lie: near enough the fine-structure constant, near enough a room number physicists of an older generation already told a joke about. She appeared, after everything, to have inherited the not-letting-lie.

Somewhere behind her a phone began ringing and did not stop for a long time. When it cut off, Jago's voice carried across the car park in fragments ("live," then, more clearly, "past a hundred now") and Lolly understood that Phase Two was still spreading while she sat on a wall discussing 1911.

"Fainrose does both," she remarked.\
"Dr Fainrose is formidable and will save perhaps nine thousand houses this week," noted de Broccoli, "but she cannot hear the question she was trained out of hearing. That is what training costs."

He picked up the hat again. "Eilstein told you there is no view from nowhere, and that nothing is definite until agreement has spread. It frightened you."\
"Yes," Lolly confirmed.\
"Good. It should. It is also not the only available account, and he did not tell you that, because he is an old snob who enjoys the vertigo." De Broccoli looked out at the car park. "May I show you the other one? It is mine, unfashionable, and I have been out of favour for eighty years, which gives a man leisure to be sure."\
"Please," Lolly urged.

"Then tell me. When Mr Flint pulled the cable from beneath Mrs Chain's bed, and the wallpaper became flowers again, where had the flowers been?"

Lolly opened her mouth and stopped.

She remembered writing it down, standing in a bedroom with two duvets and feeling the strange intellectual terror of recognition.

"They were still there," she recalled. "Underneath. Suppressed, not deleted. It was being held in place by continuous intervention."

"Yes," noted de Broccoli, quietly. "On Eilstein's account, that is loose talk. On his account there was no fact about the flowers until agreement reached them, and what you felt in that room was a metaphor."

He turned. "But you did not write down a metaphor, Ms Wren. You wrote down a mechanism. You noted the lie required electricity."\
"I did say that," Lolly confirmed.\
"It is the best sentence in your notebook, and you have not understood it yet," de Broccoli informed her.\
Lolly considered slowly, "Go on."

"Suppose the house always had one definite configuration." De Broccoli raised one gloved finger. "One actual wallpaper. An actual state, at every moment: nothing indefinite about the house itself, only about what any of us, standing outside it, were entitled to say we knew.

"And suppose there exists a wave. Not a metaphor: a real physical wave, spread over every configuration the house might have had. Every moving thing carries a matter wave, even you. Its length shortens as an object gains momentum: the [de Broglie wavelength](#glDeBroglie), vanishingly short for a person. Nobody thought to ask whether a house might have one too. Suppose the actual configuration is guided by that wave: steered, never anywhere but somewhere, yet always told where to go next by a thing that fills the whole space of alternatives."

Lolly sat very still.

"Then the unrealised versions are real," she ventured. "Even though they never happened."

"Real, and empty, and effective," mentioned de Broccoli. "That is the whole of it. The branch nobody took is not a ghost and not an accounting error. It is part of the object that decides where you go. [Interference](#glInterference) is what it feels like when an empty possibility pushes."

Lolly found, to her irritation, that she could follow him if she stopped trying to hurry the gloves. [Pilot-wave theory](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=WIyTZDHuarQ), like the Duc, is not difficult so much as unwilling to be brief.

"For an electron," Lolly noted slowly. "Not for a house."

Something moved in De Broccoli's face: the nearest yet to a smile, and not a comfortable one. "No. Not for a house. Until recently, nobody on this earth could make it matter for anything you could stand inside. That is the entire crime. Everything else is paperwork wrapped around it."

"So when Venn scanned the mantelpiece---" Lolly began.\
"Did he write a fact," de Broccoli asked, "or did he find one, and do something to it?"\
"There was already a fact."\
"Then what did he do?"

She did not answer at once. Some part of her had already worked out what he was steering her towards, and did not much want to be the one who said it first.

Lolly thought about the wallpaper. The word arrived before she chose it. "He deformed the guidance," she managed. "He altered the wave steering that woman's household. The actual configuration went somewhere thin."

De Broccoli's voice sharpened with something that might, in a younger man, have been pride. "When Mr Flint tore the cable out, the deformation ceased. The flowers came back because the wave that had always contained them was permitted, briefly, to matter again."

The air over the car park did something. Lolly chose to disbelieve it.

It happened again a moment later, smaller, as though testing whether she would admit to noticing the first time.

"That's a different crime," she countered.

"It is an entirely different crime," noted de Broccoli, "and this is the only thing I came to say." He turned to face her. "Ms Sloan is building a case that the Ministry falsified records: true, and small. Dr Fainrose is building a case that they interfered with lawful evolution: truer, and larger. But if what I have described is so, then everything they have done (the queue, the scanner, the certificates, and now the memories) was not an intrusion into knowledge."

"It was an intrusion into what steers."\
"Yes."\
"They have been standing in the wave."\
"They have been standing in the wave," reflected the Duc de Broccoli, "with clipboards, for fifteen years, charging rent."

Lolly was already writing. Her hand was not entirely steady. She did not much care about that either.

"Eilstein considered you cannot record the phase," she noted, still writing. "That the vault was worthless because there is no phase record."

"He is right."

"But if the wave is real, a physical thing in configuration space." She looked up. "It is still there. Deformed, but there. You would not restore the country from a copy. You would restore it by taking your apparatus out of the wave and letting the guidance relax."

"And the apparatus needs a way in," she continued, before he could answer. "It cannot simply reach into a house. It has to be let in." She thought of box seven, the invitation architecture, and felt something go cold and clear. "The form is not paperwork. The form is the latch."

De Broccoli did not answer at once.

When he did, his voice had changed, and Lolly realised with a small shock that the old man had not, until that moment, been certain of her.

"Ms Wren," he stated. "Nobody told you that."\
"No."\
"It does not have a name."\
"Doesn't it?"

"There are people who would recognise the shape of it," reflected de Broccoli. "There is no one who has ventured it about a country." He rose. "You will now go upstairs and say it to Dr Fainrose. She will tell you it is unfalsifiable metaphysics and a hundred years out of fashion. Then she will be unable to sleep, because it also specifies a procedure, and hers does not."

"And is it true?" asked Lolly, standing.

"I have believed it for eighty years without being able to prove it," considered the Duc de Broccoli, "which I am told is called faith, though I maintain it is merely patience."

He put out a gloved hand. Lolly shook it. It was quite cold. She did not ask why. He held on to it a moment longer than the courtesy required.
