It had not warmed by the time she let go, and she caught herself checking her own hand afterwards, absurdly, to be sure the cold had not come from somewhere else.

"Your hands are cold," she said, before she could stop herself.\
He considered this as though it were the most interesting question she had asked all morning. "Poor circulation," he said, "or else the wave arrived a half-step ahead of the particle, and the particle has not yet caught up to being warm. I have never been able to establish which. At my age, one stops insisting on a mechanism and settles for a description."

"You said you were a physicist for eleven minutes, once." He was already turning away. "I was a historian until twenty-two, and a physicist for sixty years afterwards, and never once both. That is why I could not see what your bureaucracy was doing to those houses, and you could. Do not stand on a wall about it."

He walked off across the car park. The sunlight fell on him properly, and he cast a completely ordinary shadow, for which Lolly was obscurely grateful.

She did not know if any of it was true. She could not prove a wave, or weigh one, or point to it on a form. She knew only that Fainrose was upstairs with an argument that would not survive an Inquiry at eleven, and that she held the only other one, in a green notebook. It would have to be enough. She went in.

Upstairs, Fainrose did not look up. The clock over the door said six minutes to eleven, and neither of them mentioned it, because panic would not have helped, and Fainrose had trained herself out of it years before.

"If that was the Duc," she considered, "he is a mystic and I love him and he is eighty years out of date."\
"Probably," replied Lolly. She put the notebook on the console, open. "Read it anyway."

She read it.

Then she stopped working, which she had not done in nineteen hours.

Fainrose did not say it was impossible, or ask for a citation, or reach for her own account to defend it. Lolly watched her decide to act on a thing she could not verify and did not much like, because the alternative was walking into that room with nothing. It was, Lolly thought, the whole of Dr Fainrose in one movement.

"Pilot-wave theory," Fainrose concluded at last. "Deterministic. [Nonlocal](#glNonlocality). And it comes with a repair procedure, more than my account has ever offered me." She looked up. "Lolly."

"Yes."

Rule: A pilot-wave particle is always somewhere, but the whole wave, including its empty possibilities, helps decide where it goes next.

Lesson for this chapter: [Matter Waves and Pilot-Wave Theory](#lesson-12-pilot-wave-theory)

# **Chapter 13: The Panel of Experts**

The Board of Inquiry sat at eleven in the annex conference room, and its first act was to establish that it did not intend to decide anything.

The room smelled of coffee going bitter in its pot on the sideboard and the flat photocopier-toner air of a space that had hosted a great many inquiries and concluded almost none of them.

"We are not here to adjudicate physics," announced the chair, a Mr Pilbeam, who had been brought in from Transport on the grounds that he had once managed a bridge, although the bridge had not supplied a reference. "We are here to determine whether a recommendation exists. If the expert panel is unanimous, we may act. If it is divided, the matter is unresolved and current arrangements continue."

He was a round, worried-looking man with the air of someone perpetually rehearsing a decision he had not yet been asked to make, and a tie knotted with the particular tightness of a man expecting the day to require some defending.

Somewhere above the ceiling tiles the ventilation exhaled in long, unconvinced sighs, and the wall clock ticked with the particular loudness of a clock nobody present had been told to trust.

Pilbeam kept, for reasons nobody present had the standing to question, a small and extravagantly confident terrier named Nelson, who was not permitted in the building and attended anyway, and who regarded every object in the room --- chair legs, briefcases, on one memorable occasion a visiting cat from Compliance --- as a reasonable romantic prospect.

Beatrix Sloan, at the side table with eleven boxes and a fountain pen, wrote a single word on her pad. Lolly leaned over and read it. Divided.

"They've designed it so that disagreement means Phase Two proceeds," she said.

"They have designed it," mentioned Beatrix, "so that nothing is ever anybody's fault. It is the same instrument every time. Convene the ones who cannot agree, and call the noise a lack of evidence."

"Who did they convene," Fainrose asked.

Beatrix turned her pad round. Three names. Fainrose read them and put her face in her hands, briefly, which Lolly had not seen before. "Oh, that's marvellous," she managed. "That's genuinely inspired. They've picked three men who have spent their entire lives disagreeing about exactly this, and one of them is dead."\
"Which one," Lolly asked.\
"It has not noticeably impeded his work," Fainrose replied.

Nobody in the room said anything while they waited, which was its own kind of information. Pilbeam checked his watch twice, as though a third glance might produce a different number.

They came in at ten past. The first was tall, hollow-cheeked, immaculately grey, his posture that of someone who had spent decades being the cleverest person in dangerous rooms. He carried nothing: no papers, no case, choosing the seat furthest from the window.

Beyond the glass, wedged between two other Ministry wings, ran a strip of sky the exact grey of a form still awaiting a signature.

He carried no scent either --- no coffee, no chalk dust, nothing --- and in a room thick with nervous sweat and photocopier toner, his was the one presence that gave nothing away.

He did not look at anyone as he sat down, and yet somehow contrived to give the impression that he had already read everyone's file, reached a verdict, and filed it away privately.

"Professor Werner Heisenburger," considered Pilbeam. "Foundations."\
"Formalism," noted Heisenburger. "Not foundations. There is a difference and it is the entire difference."

He said it in the same flat monotone he brought to every sentence, each word given exactly the same weight as the last, as though emphasis were a variable he had long ago set to zero.

The second arrived talking. He was younger by decades, sandy-haired, tablet in one hand and coffee in the other. "---if the equations are the ontology then this whole crisis is a bookkeeping dispute --- sorry, hello, Max Tengelman, cosmology, I've read the Fainrose paper, Dr Fainrose it's an honour, your syndrome table is gorgeous."

He had a rumpled energy, as though he had dressed mid-argument with himself: collar done up one hole wrong, shirt untucked on one side.

Fainrose instructed, "Sit down, Professor Tengelman."\
"Absolutely."

The third came in last and quietly, and the room's temperature dropped about it. He was a smallish man with a red beard going white, in a jumper, with the bright unblinking gaze of somebody who has spent thirty years being polite to people he considers sloppy. His Belfast accent had gone flat with decades of being right in rooms that resented it, and he sat down without shaking hands.

"Dr Jack Bellboy," remarked Pilbeam.\
"Aye," reflected Bellboy.\
"Dr Bellboy, forgive me, our records indicate---" Pilbeam tried again.\
"They do," Bellboy replied. "Shall we get on."

No one offered him coffee, though a full pot sat within reach on the sideboard. No one seemed to think of it, and afterwards no one could quite say why not.

Nobody pursued it. Pilbeam distributed the question. It had been drafted by a committee and ran to a paragraph, but stripped of its clothing it asked this: does the Ministry's programme of civic simplification cause damage to physical reality that is not captured by the loss of records?

Heisenburger answered first, and answered fast. "No," he stated. "And the question is malformed."\
Pilbeam raised his eyes, hopeful. "You find no damage?"

"I find no meaning." Heisenburger's hands stayed folded. "The formalism relates preparations to observations. It tells you what your instruments will report. It says nothing about what the electron is doing when nobody asks, because that question generates no numbers. You do not need to picture it. You need to trust the arithmetic and read the dial." He turned his head slightly. "Your survey teams asked; they received answers; the answers were correct. Unkindness is politics. A hidden variable in the house is a fiction."

Lolly blurted, before she could stop herself, "The woman couldn't finish her sister's name."

Heisenburger looked at her with no unkindness at all, which was worse. "Then she was harmed," he replied. "I do not dispute the harm. I dispute the ontology of the harm. She was harmed as a citizen, by a procedure, and you should prosecute the procedure. Do not ask me to certify that a wave function was injured."

Tengelman had his hand half up. "See, this is where I get off the bus," he said. "You say the question generates no numbers. But the equation generates numbers, and my position, a minority one, is that the mathematical structure isn't a description of reality, it is the reality."

Pilbeam wrote this down, visibly hoping relevance might emerge if given enough ruled paper.

"And so?" Fainrose asked.\
"And so nothing was destroyed," Tengelman replied.

The room went very quiet.

"Say that again," noted Beatrix.

"Nothing was destroyed." He reflected it gently, and Lolly understood he was neither stupid nor cruel, which was worse. "If the equation never stops, and there's no [collapse](#glCollapse), the universe didn't pick one answer and delete the rest. It branched. Photograph and clock."

Then he spread his hands. "The alternatives weren't extinguished. They were [decohered](#glDecoherence) from. Everything this Ministry allegedly destroyed still exists. It's just not here."

"That's the Many-Worlds picture," noted Beatrix, without looking up from her pad. "Councils don't minute universes."

Mrs Elspeth Chain had come in during this, with the umbrella, and stood at the back, by the door. She asked, "Then where is my sister."

Tengelman turned, and to his credit he did not flinch. "She's in the branches where the record wasn't overwritten," he said. "Which is most of them, statistically---"

"Where is she here."\
"\...Here, she's gone. Yes."\
"Then do not tell me nothing was destroyed," stated Mrs Chain, "because I am here, and I am the one who noticed."

There was a silence of some length.

"That's fair," mentioned Max Tengelman quietly. "That's actually a fair objection and it's the one I've never had a good answer to."

Beatrix Sloan was already writing, fast. "Chair," she declared. "I want it minuted that a member of the expert panel has advanced a position under which no act of the Ministry can constitute destruction of anything."

Pilbeam brightened. "Well, that would be---"\
"That would be the defence," interjected Beatrix, before he could finish. "That is what they convened him for: a physics under which the harm is relocated rather than caused. You will see it in the Board's response: no alternative is ever lost, and therefore no citizen has suffered a loss, and therefore Phase Two may proceed."

Tengelman put his coffee down. "I'd hate that," he protested. "For the record, I'd hate that. It's a real misuse. The multiverse doesn't make cruelty free. My branch still has the crying person in it---"

Up close he smelled of burnt coffee and the faint ozone tang of a tablet that had been running hot in his pocket all morning.

"It does not matter what you would hate," corrected Beatrix. "It matters what you have said in a minuted proceeding."\
"Yeah," admitted Tengelman. "Yeah, I know. That's --- yeah."

And it was into this that Jack Bellboy spoke for the second time. "Now that," he said, "is the whole disease, and the two of you have just performed it for me, so I'm obliged."

Everyone looked at him. Bellboy had not moved. "Werner says the question has no meaning. Max says it has too many answers, all of them real. Between the pair of you there's not one thing a Board of Inquiry could go and check." He tapped the table once. "You've both got theories in which nobody can ever be wrong. I've spent my career objecting to that."

Heisenburger stated, "The formalism is confirmed to twelve decimal places."

"The formalism is grand," Bellboy replied. "I'm talking about the word. Measurement. It sounds like a special class of event the rest of nature is exempt from. There isn't. A surveyor is a physical system. A form is a physical system. There is no magic in a clipboard."

Lolly asked, "Then what did happen in Mrs Chain's house."

And Bellboy turned to her, and Lolly had the disconcerting experience of being properly surveyed at by somebody who had died before he had finished caring about this. "You're the woman with the notebook."\
"Yes."\
"The Duc's been at you."\
"This morning."\
"Aye, he does that." Something almost like affection crossed Bellboy's face and left again. "Right. Here's your answer, and it's the only part of this morning worth putting in the minutes."

Before he held up his fingers, Bellboy asked a question instead. "Answer me something first, Mr Chairman. When you're not looking at your desk, do you believe it's still there?"

Somewhere under the table, Nelson had located the ankle of a representative from Compliance and was pursuing the matter with a dedication the Board itself had never quite managed. Pilbeam blinked. "\...Yes?"

"Congratulations. You're a realist, and you made the choice without noticing you'd made one." Bellboy held the two fingers up properly now. "There are two things a reasonable person wants to believe. One: things have properties whether or not anybody's asking. That's realism, and you've just admitted to it. Two: what you do here cannot instantly affect what's true over there --- no influence outrunning light. Call that [locality](#glLocality)."

He lowered the fingers. "Every sensible person in this room wants both. Werner avoids the problem by giving up the first. Max keeps both by multiplying the worlds until the question dissolves. And in 1964 I sat down and did a small piece of arithmetic, and the arithmetic said: you cannot have both. Not as a matter of taste. As a matter of measurable numbers."

Pilbeam, faintly: "Is this --- forgive me --- is this your theorem, Dr Bellboy?"

"It has my name on it," noted Bellboy, "which was never my idea and isn't my fault. [Bell's theorem](#glBellstheorem), if it makes the minutes happier. I'd have called it [an inequality somebody was bound to write down eventually](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=w_fK3EBPNb8)."

"Measurable," Pilbeam echoed.

"That's the point of it, Mr Chairman." Bellboy leaned forward. "If both things were true (properties in advance, no influence at a distance), then certain correlations have a ceiling. Four settings, run it enough times, and you get a single number. Local realism caps it at two. Call it [the CHSH inequality](#glCHSH), if one name wasn't enough for one morning. And if nature ever exceeds that ceiling, then one of your two comfortable beliefs is finished."

"And has it been done," Beatrix asked.

"It's been done for sixty years," Bellboy continued, "in Berkeley and Orsay and Innsbruck and Delft and Vienna, tighter and tighter, and every hole anybody found in it has been plugged, and they gave a Nobel Prize for it in 2022, and the answer is the same every time and it isn't close."

He sat back. "The ceiling is violated. That's nonlocality: nature is not locally real. It is not philosophy and it is not fashion and it does not depend on which interpretation you take home at night. It is an experimental fact and it is the only weapon in this building."

And it does not stop at two. Quantum mechanics has a ceiling of its own above the classical one (two root two, and not a hair further), worked out by a man who wanted to know why nature couldn't be pushed harder than that. "Ask a committee for [the Tsirelson bound](#glTsirelson)," Bellboy said, "and watch them hope it's a typo."

"Of course it has a ceiling," said Beatrix, not looking up from her pad. "Even the universe has a compliance limit."\
Fainrose had gone extremely still. "Say the last part again," she managed.

"You've been told it's metaphysics," Bellboy continued, "and that her guiding wave is unfalsifiable, and both dismissals are true and both are beside the point." He nodded towards Lolly. "You don't need to win the interpretation. You need one thing in front of Mr Pilbeam, the one thing tested to death: that a change compelled in one place can alter what is true in another, with no signal between them. You don't need to feel why it's true. You need to trust the number, and go and get it."

"Where would you put a probe," Fainrose asked, low, "if you could put it anywhere in the district?"

Lolly felt the whole architecture of three days slide into position with an almost audible click. "The overflow channel," she breathed.

"Go on."

"They tied nine thousand dwellings to one anchor. Fainrose's correlation lattice." She was standing now, without having decided to. "The Board's position is that each dwelling was surveyed separately, so each certificate is separately defensible. Local decisions. Local harms. Nine thousand small administrative matters, each too small to be worth an inquiry."

"And?"

"And if the correlations exceed Bellboy's ceiling," Lolly continued, "then they weren't separate. It's a number. You can measure it in Sub-District 6 this afternoon and get a figure that no committee can call metaphysics. It just says: if these were independent systems being independently surveyed, this correlation is impossible."

Bellboy smiled for the first time, and it was not a warm expression, it was the smile of a man watching a tool being picked up by the correct end. "There you are," he approved. "Now you've got a case."

Heisenburger noted, quietly, "The inequality does not tell you what is real."

"It does not," Bellboy agreed. "It tells you what is not permitted. That's a smaller claim and it's the reason it can't be argued with. Werner, you spent your life saying we should confine ourselves to what can be checked. I took you at your word and went and checked something, and you've never forgiven me for it."

"I have forgiven you," reflected Heisenburger. "I have never agreed with you." A pause. "There is a difference, and after everything, it is the only distinction I have left that I am still entitled to."

Something passed across the old man's face (Lolly could not read it, and thought later that she perhaps could) and Heisenburger folded his hands again and said nothing further for the remainder of the proceeding.

Tengelman ventured, "Jack. If they measure it and it violates, my picture survives that."\
"It does," Bellboy confirmed. "Your picture survives everything, Max. In a less robust theory that would be an administrative concern."

"No," replied Tengelman, with real cheerfulness, "but I'll take it. And for what it's worth---" he turned to Mrs Chain, and did it properly, and looked at her while he said it. "Nothing being destroyed globally doesn't buy anybody anything locally. Somebody in this branch lost her sister. I'll put that in the minutes."

"Thank you," Mrs Chain acknowledged.

Fainrose's voice cut back in, flat. "One thing before the minutes close. A CHSH run on a live lattice isn't a telescope. You don't read nine thousand dwellings from a safe distance: you send a coincidence probe through the same anchor that already took Mrs Chain's sister out of this branch. Do it carelessly, and you don't get a number. You get another empty chair."

A different silence from the last one.

No one moved to break it. Beatrix's fountain pen stayed uncapped above the page, the ink drying by degrees, and still she did not write.

Mrs Chain did not move from the door. "Then do it carefully," she decided. "But do it."\
Beatrix looked up properly, for the first time in several minutes. "It is not without risk, Mrs Chain."

"I did not ask whether it was without risk." Mrs Chain's voice did not rise. "I asked whether it would tell you something true. Do it in my house first, if a house is wanted. There is nothing left in it I am not already braced to lose."

Pilbeam, faintly: "That's --- that's very public spirited."

"It is not public spirited," corrected Mrs Chain. "It is arithmetic. Somebody's kitchen was always going to be the one. It may as well be mine, since I am already standing in the doorway."

Pilbeam, who had understood perhaps a third of the last twenty minutes but had grasped with the unerring instinct of his species that a decision was forming, asked, "So is the panel unanimous?"

"No," reflected all three of them.

Pilbeam sagged.

"But we're agreed on a measurement," ventured Bellboy. "Which is worth ten unanimous opinions and you may minute that too. Werner will accept it because it's an instrument reading. Max will accept it because his branch has instruments in it. I'll accept it because I designed it. And whatever number comes back, none of the three of us can wriggle."

Beatrix allowed herself, for one breath, something that in a less controlled woman might have been called a smile. "They built an instrument for manufacturing doubt," she said. "You have just told it to manufacture a number instead. I do not believe that is a design fault they anticipated."

She laid down her pen. "Chair. The panel proposes a test with a pre-agreed threshold and three incompatible experts bound in advance to accept the result. Refusing that is not caution. It is obstruction, and I will write it up as obstruction, and I will name the committee that would not give its members' names." She paused. "I will also note, for the file, that the Board reconvenes in ten days to ratify Phase Two by default, absent a formal objection entered with evidence. That clock is already running, whether or not anyone signs this. Refusing the test does not stop it. It only decides who is left unopposed when it goes."

Numbers have the administrative advantage of not caring who chairs the panel. Mr Pilbeam, who had chaired several universes' worth of them, recognised this immediately and looked, for a moment, almost grateful.

Pilbeam eyed round the table for somebody to save him and found nobody. "Very well," he conceded. "Minute it."

The door opened before anyone could rise, and a man came in who did not so much enter the room as fail to be noticed entering it.

He was of perfectly average height, in a suit of no memorable colour, and he carried a slim folder that he never opened.

"I have no business with this Board," he stated, "and no obligation to explain who sent me."\
"Is this about the black hole," put in Beatrix, not looking up from her pad.

"I can neither confirm that phrase nor discuss it productively." He set the folder, still closed, on the table. "Everyone present will need to complete Form 6/G before I go. It asks what you know. Leave blank anything you're unsure you're permitted to know."

Nelson had by now identified his trouser leg as a subject worth investigating, and was investigating it with some thoroughness. The man did not look down, and did not appear to notice.

"Are you here to help," asked Lolly, "or to establish that something is happening?"\
"That distinction," he replied, "is itself something I'm not cleared to draw."

He reclaimed his ankle without visible resentment, collected the folder, unopened still, and paused at the door. "This meeting did not take place. I'd be grateful if the minutes reflected that."

"They already don't," noted Beatrix, and resumed writing them.

He left. Nobody had learned his name, his department, or a single fact about Sub-District 6 they had not already possessed on his arrival.

Pilbeam let out a breath he had apparently been rationing since the man arrived. "Should we be worried?"\
"I'm not cleared to say," replied Beatrix, without inflection, and went back to the minutes.\
Afterwards, in the corridor, Bellboy stopped Lolly with two fingers on her sleeve. "A word."\
"Yes."

"Your Duc's account and Max's account and Werner's silence: you'll be tempted to pick one." The pale eyes were unblinking. "Don't pick yet. Until then hold all three, and use whichever one tells you where to put the probe. That's not cowardice, that's method."

"And if none of them tells me?"\
"Then you've found the good part," noted Jack Bellboy, "and you'd better write it down."

He walked off down the corridor and Lolly did not watch him go, on the grounds that she was not entirely certain she would see it.

She opened the notebook instead.

She wrote:

Properties in advance, or no influence at a distance. Choose one.

Nature has already chosen, and it is measurable.

Then:

I do not have to win the interpretation. I have to produce a number

the committee cannot call philosophy.

And beneath that:

They surveyed nine thousand houses separately. If the correlations

exceed the ceiling, then there were never nine thousand cases. There

was one.

And beneath that:

Ten days. Mrs Chain's house first, because she asked for it to be

hers. Then Sub-District 6, and whatever the number costs to get.

"Get the number," offered Bellboy, from a doorway that was no longer occupied. "Then you may philosophise on your own time."

Rule: Local innocence cannot explain the correlations; a number can show that, a committee cannot talk it away, and a clock, once running, does not stop for anybody's objection but the evidence's.

Lesson for this chapter: [Bell's Theorem and the CHSH Inequality](#lesson-13-bells-theorem)

# **Chapter 14: Eleven Dimensions and a Whisper**

They went into Sub-District 6 on the Thursday with two vans, a portable timing rack, four hired photodetectors, and paperwork Beatrix had signed in a hand that dared anyone to query it. Fainrose was in the primary school hall running cable. Jago had gone for a fifth detector. Lolly, sent out for exceeding her quota of useful questions, ended up in the Coldharrow Street library with the green notebook and eleven photocopied pages from box seven.

Lolly thought of Fainrose's line about forty-one clipboards and a stubborn moon, back before any of this had a name. Sub-District 6 did not have forty-one houses. It had considerably more, and no moon to blame.

There was one other person in the reading room: a slight man in his seventies, entirely bald, papers colonising two additional tables, pausing every twenty minutes to look at the shut-down carpet showroom. The librarian had given up asking him to consolidate, and simply reshelved around him. His pencil, resharpened so many times with the little penknife he kept for the purpose, was barely the length of a finger; his hand was so small and dense that a page of his notes looked, from across the table, like a piece of knitting.

At about four o'clock he observed, without turning round, in a voice so quiet that Lolly was not initially certain it had been aimed at her: "You are the person doing the Bell test on the housing lattice."

Lolly put down her pen. "How do you know that."

"Dr Bellboy mentioned it." The man turned. He had a pleasant, unemphatic face and the mildest possible manner, and he spoke so softly that Lolly found herself leaning forward. "I think it is an excellent idea. It is the correct instinct. I ought to say who I am before I say anything else. Eduard von Wittenberg."

He smelled of old paper and cold radiator dust --- the kind of smell that settles into a man after decades spent among books.

"Lolly Wren."\
"Yes," he replied, gathering three sheets of paper together, apparently at random, and coming to sit down opposite her. "May I ask you something before you ask me anything?"\
"Of course."

"When you say the country is held together by correlations" --- he offered it gently, as though it might be fragile --- "what do you imagine the correlations are between?"

Lolly thought about it. "Houses," she said. "Records. Routes. Districts. People. The nodes on Fainrose's lattice."\
"Yes. And what are those made of."

"Bricks. Paper. Copper. People are formed of cells." She heard where this was going. "Cells are made of molecules, molecules of atoms, atoms of nuclei and electrons, nuclei of quarks."

Not, she reminded herself, the sort that came in a tub with a spoon. A small, private joke, and she kept it to herself.

"And quarks?"\
"Are wrought of nothing. They're fundamental. Points."

Von Wittenberg smiled very slightly. "That is the standard answer and it is the best-tested answer in the history of thought," he continued. "It is also, I believe, wrong, and I have believed it for about fifty years and been unable to prove it."

Lolly sat back. "You're going to tell me they're [strings](#glString)."

"I am going to tell you something a little worse than that," noted Eduard von Wittenberg apologetically. He looked at the green notebook lying between them and said nothing, waiting, until she turned it to a clean page herself and slid it towards him. "I find I use my hands better than my voice, for this part."

He drew a tiny dot. "A point particle," he explained. "It has no size and no internal structure. If you wish to explain why there are so many different particles, you must simply list them."

Beneath the dot he drew a tiny closed loop. "Now suppose instead that the fundamental object is not a point but a small loop, of a length so short that every experiment ever performed sees it as a point. A loop can do something a point cannot." He looked up. "It can vibrate."

"Different vibrations, different particles," Lolly said.

"Different vibrations, different particles. Not seventeen unrelated facts. One object, seventeen notes." He said it without any drama whatever, which somehow made it land harder. "A violin string is not seventeen instruments. It is one string, played differently."

Lolly studied the loop for a while. "And the electron and the quark and the photon---"\
"Notes."\
"And gravity?"

"Ah," von Wittenberg replied. "That is the part that persuaded me. For fifty years the scandal was that our theory of the very small and our theory of gravity would not sit in the same room. Put them together and the answers came out infinite. Every attempt failed."

He tapped the little loop. "But if the objects are loops rather than points, the infinities do not arise. And more than that: among the notes the loop can play, one of them, unavoidably, is a massless spin-two particle."

"Which is?"

The pencil did not move. For the length of one breath, neither did he.

"The graviton," explained von Wittenberg. "You do not put gravity in. You cannot keep it out. It is simply one of the things the string does." He paused. "I have never got over that. I am seventy-four and I have not got over it."

Lolly realised she had stopped taking notes and started again. "Where's the catch."

"There are three, and I shall not hide them." Von Wittenberg counted them off on his fingers. "First: the strings are so small that no accelerator we could build could see one directly. There has never been a direct experimental test. Dr Fainrose is entirely right to say so."

"Second?"

"The mathematics does not work in four dimensions. It requires ten." He said this the way another man might mention that a recipe requires buttermilk. "Nine of space, one of time. Since we experience three, the other six must be curled up small --- wound so tightly that nothing you or I can do will ever push anything along them."

Lolly peered out of the window at the carpet showroom. "That is a great deal to ask of a car park," she remarked.

Somewhere outside, a car alarm went off and was silenced. Lolly glanced at her watch, a new habit, three days old, and found that two hours had gone, with 4C still losing mass somewhere behind all of this.

It had gone quiet in a way she was starting to distrust. Nobody had come to fetch her, which these days felt less like a mercy than a delay of judgement.

"It is, yes." Von Wittenberg looked pleased. "And the third catch is why I am in a library in Sub-District 6 rather than at home. For a long time there were five different string theories. An embarrassment: one had set out to explain why there is only one world and had produced five candidates."

"So what happened."

"In 1995," began Eduard von Wittenberg, "I gave a talk." He said it so quietly and so flatly that Lolly nearly missed the size of it.

"The five theories were not five theories," mentioned von Wittenberg. "They were five descriptions of one structure, related by [dualities](#glDuality). Assembled properly, they did not fit in ten dimensions. They fitted in eleven, and the objects were not only strings but membranes."

"And that's the theory."\
"That is the theory, and it has no proper formulation yet, and so it has no proper name. I called it [M-theory](#glMtheory)\
."\
"What does the M stand for."

"Membrane," replied von Wittenberg. "Or mystery. Or magic. According to taste. I have never settled it, and I have come to think the vagueness was accidentally honest."

Lolly closed the notebook. "Professor von Wittenberg," she stated. "I will write down anything that tells me what they dumped into 4C. I will not take dictation on eleven dimensions until then. In three days I have been told four incompatible accounts of what is real. And you have now told me everything is made of eleven-dimensional membranes nobody can see."

She looked at the little loop. "I have a woman in this district who was stopped in the middle of remembering her sister's name. What am I supposed to do with strings?"

Von Wittenberg did not take offence. "That is the right question," he noted. "First: nothing. You are supposed to do nothing with strings. The Bell measurement is what matters this week. Anyone who tells you eleven dimensions will help you get an injunction is lying to you."

"All right."

Lolly had not realised how much she needed someone to say "I do not have a tool for this week" in a library. It improved every subsequent expert. Honesty, it turned out, was a measurement basis of its own.

"Second: I did not come here to tell you what everything is fashioned of. I cannot demonstrate it." He turned one sheet round. "I came because of your black [hole](#glBlackHole)."

Lolly looked down at the sheet. It was covered in an extremely dense and entirely orderly hand, and among the symbols she could make out almost nothing, but at the top, underlined once, was written:

> 4C: HORIZON ENTROPY: WHERE IS THE LEDGER

Her mouth went dry. "Eilstein," she breathed.

"Albrecht telephoned me on Tuesday and shouted at me for eleven minutes." Von Wittenberg's mouth twitched. "Eleven. I did not choose the number, and I confess I noticed it before I noticed anything he actually said. His concern is that your Ministry has used a small engineered black hole as a disposal channel for suppressed alternatives, that the object is evaporating, and that when it has gone there will be no record of what was destroyed. He calls this the falsification of the ledger. The Ministry, I imagine, would prefer the word reclassification."

It was, Lolly thought, a tidy word for something that had not asked anyone's permission to disappear.

"And you can tell him it's all right?"

"No," von Wittenberg replied. "But I can tell you why he may be wrong, and it is the one thing my subject has actually achieved." He turned the sheet over. "A black hole has [an entropy](#glBlackholearealaw)," he continued. "Bekenstein saw it and Hawking made it precise: the entropy is proportional to the area of the horizon. Not to the volume inside. To the area of the surface."

He let that sit for a moment. "That was extremely strange, and for twenty years nobody could say what it was counting. Entropy counts states. Which states? Of what? The black hole was supposed to have almost no properties at all."

"And?"

"In 1996 two colleagues took a particular class of black hole in [string theory](#glString), [counted the configurations of D-branes, and found](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=k6TWO-ESC6A) that the number of states gave exactly the Bekenstein-Hawking entropy. Exactly. With the correct factor." He noted it, quietly. "That is the first time anyone counted the microscopic states of a black hole and got the entropy formula back, digit for digit."

Lolly waited for the qualification that always followed a claim that size. It did not come.

Lolly replied slowly, "So there are states. It isn't featureless."\
"It is not featureless. It has an enormous number of internal configurations, and the horizon area measures how many."\
"Then when it evaporates---"

"Then the question becomes whether the radiation carries them out," said von Wittenberg. "The area law suggests something outrageous: that everything inside a region can be encoded on its boundary. The account is on the surface. There is a precise version in which gravity in a certain space is equivalent to a theory without gravity on its edge. Two descriptions. Same physics."

Lolly sat completely still. "Then the ledger isn't inside it," she breathed.

"That is what I came to say," von Wittenberg confirmed. "The record of what fell in, of what they dumped, isn't in the interior waiting to be annihilated when the interior stops existing. It's on the horizon."

She was writing now, fast and badly. "And the horizon is exactly what the radiation comes out of."

"Yes," considered Eduard von Wittenberg. "And so the current view, not proven, but the view, is that evaporation does not destroy the information. It is scrambled almost beyond recovery, but it is not annihilated. Unitarity survives. The books balance."

He put the sheet down. "Tell Albrecht that the ledger is not kept in the vault. It is kept on the wall."\
Lolly looked up. "Do you believe it?"

"I believe it the way de Broccoli believes his wave," von Wittenberg replied. "Which is to say: I have thought about it for decades, the mathematics is far too good to be an accident, and I cannot show it to you. There's a line of Feynman's I've never managed to improve on. He said he wasn't going to explain a thing in a way you could understand, because he didn't understand it that way himself: he was going to explain it the way physicists calculate it, and it works. That is my position exactly, and I have had rather longer than he did to be embarrassed about it. Dr Bellboy would give me no credit at all, and he would be right to."

He gathered his papers. "This is the difficulty with being in my line of work. We have produced, I think, the most beautiful structure in the history of physics, and we have not produced a single number a committee could check. Your Bell test will produce one in two days. I am aware which of us is more useful this week."

He stood, and did not sit back down. "One thing more, and it is not physics."\
"Please."

"I was a history major. I intended to be a journalist. I came to physics late and by an unusual road, as did de Broccoli, as did Bellboy, in his fashion."

He picked up his pencil. "I mention it only because everybody who has come to talk to you this week arrived from somewhere else, and I do not think that is a coincidence. The people who ask what a thing is are usually people who were not trained to take it for granted."

He went back to his table, and to his papers, and did not speak again for the rest of the afternoon.

At half past six Lolly walked back to the school hall through streets that were still visibly deciding things, and found Fainrose sitting on a crate eating a fish paste sandwich of indeterminate age beside a rack of timing electronics.

"You've been with von Wittenberg," she remarked. "You've got the expression. Everyone gets the expression."

"He says everything is eleven-dimensional membranes."\
"Yes."\
"And that the record of everything the Ministry dumped into 4C is written on the horizon, not inside it, so it can't be destroyed by evaporation."

Fainrose stopped chewing. "Say that again," she instructed.

Lolly opened the notebook and showed her the page. Fainrose read it twice.

"That's not a defence," she countered, and it was not what Lolly had expected at all. "Do you know what a building like this actually listens to, Lolly? Not evidence. Not even a good argument. A deadline." She was already on her feet. "If the information's on the horizon, it comes out in the radiation, over the whole remaining lifetime of the object. Which means there's a window. And the window closes when 4C does."

"How long have we got?"

"I told you once it would be single-digit hours," Fainrose replied, before Lolly could ask again. "I was extrapolating from a feed rate that assumed nobody was about to make matters worse. I was wrong, and I have had three days to sit with that."

"Nobody knows. That's rather the point of it." Fainrose said it the way she said most unwelcome things: flatly, and already moving. "Nobody can tell you when a black hole finishes evaporating, only that it will, and that it goes faster the smaller it gets. Could be years. Could be a great deal less. We don't choose the schedule, and we don't get sent a copy." She looked at Lolly properly for the first time since she'd sat down. "Lolly. How long have we been treating the evaporation as the catastrophe?"

"Three days."

"It isn't the catastrophe. It's the disclosure. That thing is reading out everything they ever put into it, in the most scrambled handwriting there is, and it stops when it finishes burning. Nobody in this building set that clock, and nobody outside it can read it yet." She was already reaching for the nearest crate of cable. "We'd better learn fast. Starting tonight: I want something on that horizon that never blinks, and I want it there before anyone remembers to stop us."

Lolly sat down slowly on the crate Fainrose had vacated, and did not, for once, ask what she was supposed to do with strings. Writing it down was, this week, the only kind of acting she had learned to trust; so she opened the notebook and wrote:

One object. Many notes.

Five theories that were one theory seen from five sides.

Then:

The entropy goes as the area, not the volume. The account is kept on the surface.

And beneath it, underlined twice:

The evaporation is not the destruction of the record. It is the

reading of it, aloud, once, at increasing speed, and nobody has been

listening.

The first detector clicked.

Then the second.

Then all four together.

Lolly stopped writing.

"That's not a signal," noted Fainrose, already at the rack.\
"What is it?" Lolly asked.\
Fainrose looked at the trace. "It's the apparatus measuring itself."

Rule: If the apparatus shares the Ministry's clock, it will measure the Ministry and call the agreement a result.

Lesson for this chapter: [String Theory and M-Theory](#lesson-14a-string-theory-and-m-theory)

→ [Black-Hole Entropy and Holography](#lesson-14b-entropy-and-holography)

# **Chapter 15: Final State Pending**

The fault took a day to find and an afternoon to starve.

What the four detectors had turned out to agree about, in the end, was not physics. It was the time. Every timing rack the Ministry owned drew its clock from the same civic distribution. The two ends of a Bell test are supposed to be strangers who have never met, each choosing its question in the last possible instant, with no way for one side's choice to leak into the other's. Hers had been sharing a heartbeat: the same feed, the same random setting-choices, the same civic second ticking through both stations like a rumour. Between them the two ends had produced a beautiful, stable and entirely worthless agreement: the apparatus, measuring the Ministry, and mistaking the echo for a result.

"It is the cleanest result I have ever recorded," Fainrose said, "it means nothing whatsoever, and had I taken it to a committee they would have funded three more."

Lolly had the shape of it before the explanation finished, because by then she had the shape of it everywhere. "It's Venn's scanner. The instrument was inside the thing it was measuring."

"The instrument was inside the thing it was measuring," Fainrose agreed, "and the instrument was ours, so nobody thought to look."

The repair was not clever. It was subtraction, which by now Lolly understood to be the only move the whole business had ever actually rewarded. They cut the rig loose from anything with a crest on it. Two clocks instead of one: a pair of cheap oscillators left to run free and deliberately out of step, one at each station. The setting-choices now came off two smoke-detector sources Gideon had signed out of the annex stores, on the principle that radioactive decay does not wait for a central feed, does not consult a rota, and has never once been caught producing the answer anybody wanted. Jago left the second detector rack in a van on the ridge with a car battery and a young man from the annex whose entire brief was to change nothing. "Congratulations," Jago told him, signing the handover chit with a flourish, "you are tonight's most important idiot in physics. Touch nothing, radio if it rains, and if anyone official asks, you've never met me, this van, or Bell." What the rig did, once it was properly deaf, was put the same two questions to a pair of houses at opposite ends of Fainrose's lattice, houses the Ministry had surveyed as separate cases, on separate afternoons, in separate folders, over and over, in an order neither end could predict, and count how often their answers agreed.

They agreed far too often.

"That's the one Bell minded about most," noted Bellboy, who had come out to the hall to watch a trace he had no need to watch, being both dead and certain. "Not the detectors. The choices. If something you cannot see is deciding what gets measured and which measurement gets made, the argument folds and you never learn that it folded. He worried about it to the end. So should you."

They ran it clean on the Sunday. The number Bellboy had promised in the conference room came up a little after four.

Nobody moved while the display finished its refresh cycle. Four seconds is a remarkably long time to spend not breathing next to a timing rack.

Fainrose read it off the timing computer with no expression at all, which was how Lolly knew it was real. "Two point four one. Plus or minus three in the last place."

"Ceiling's two," noted Bellboy.\
"The ceiling is two. The threshold we put in front of Pilbeam in writing was two point three, error bars clear of it."

"Then you're clear of it." He studied the trace a moment longer. "That is the CHSH number: the four-setting bet I spent the back half of my career defending. Local realism cannot clear two. Quantum mechanics is licensed to two root two (Tsirelson's wall) and nobody has scratched it in seventy years of trying. A change driven in one place altering what is true in another, and nothing crossing between: that is nonlocal, and proven, and older than any of you. Nine thousand houses, surveyed one at a time, answering as one thing. Werner cannot call that metaphysics: it is an instrument reading, and instrument readings are the only things he has ever accepted. Max cannot call it harmless: it is his instrument reading too. There is no interpretation anywhere in the figure. That is precisely why it will hold."

Fainrose did not pack the rig away afterwards. She moved one station indoors, retuned it from Bellboy's cross-lattice correlation to the district's own internal one, and set it to log. "That one stays," she noted. "Different pair, different number. It will sit flat and stupid for as long as the anchor is still listening to these houses. The morning it starts to climb is the morning we have actually let go."

Lolly wrote the legal number in the green notebook, and then the sentence beneath it that mattered more:

You cannot test a thing with an instrument that shares its clock. The

apparatus was part of the Ministry, so of course it agreed with the

Ministry.

And under that:

They surveyed nine thousand houses separately. The houses answered as

one. That is now a measured fact rather than an argument, and it is

the only sentence in this entire affair that a court cannot talk its

way around.

She telephoned Beatrix at six and was told to say the number. She said it. Beatrix hung up without another word, which from Beatrix was effusive.

Beatrix Sloan won the injunction application on the Monday morning and lost the argument about what to do with it by lunchtime, because the Board, faced with an order it could not ignore, did what boards do when cornered: it asked for a plan, in writing, by four o'clock, confident that no plan could possibly exist and that confidence, once minuted, would constitute a defence. It had worked before.

"They think we're bluffing," said Beatrix, setting down the telephone with the particular gentleness of a woman who would have preferred to throw it.\
"We are not bluffing," remarked Fainrose. "We simply do not yet have a plan."\
"That is the same thing to a Board, for four hours and two biscuits."

Lolly Wren, who had spent the walk back from the courthouse turning something over in the green notebook that she could not yet make into sentences, announced, "We have four plans. We have never had fewer than four."

Everyone looked at her.

Nobody spoke. It is a particular kind of quiet a room makes when it suspects the youngest person in it is about to be right.

"Eilstein: spread better agreement. De Broccoli: take the apparatus out of the wave. Fainrose: repair from surviving correlations, in the right order. Von Wittenberg: listen to the horizon before it finishes burning."

"Those are four different physics," noted Fainrose, "held by four people who have never once agreed on anything else."

"Yes," Lolly replied. "And Bellboy told me not to pick until something forced me to, and to use whichever one told me where to put the probe."

She closed the notebook. "Nothing has forced me to pick. So I'm not going to. I'm going to use all four, because none of them actually contradicts what we do with a cable and a timing rack on an ordinary working afternoon. They only contradict each other about what it means. Nobody has ever asked us to certify what it means. The injunction asks us to fix nine thousand houses in eleven weeks."

Fainrose was quiet for a moment, in the stillness that Lolly now knew to wait out rather than fill.

"And if I asked you which of the four is actually true?" she wondered at last.

Lolly felt the old pull immediately --- towards the green notebook, towards one more careful page of distinctions before she had to commit to anything. She let her hand rest flat on the desk instead, where she could feel it not reaching.

"Then you'd be asking me to pick a religion instead of write a repair manual," Lolly replied. "So don't ask me that. Ask me what happens on Tuesday morning."

Fainrose almost smiled. "Reality is real enough to kill you if you step in front of a bus. Nobody has ever needed an interpretation for that."

"Say the plan," Fainrose instructed. "Slowly. I want to write it down exactly once."

She offered the plan slowly.

Von Wittenberg's people would listen at the horizon for whatever of the four hundred and eleven P-9 states could still be pulled from the radiation before the anchor finished evaporating. That deadline would not move.

Fainrose's team would treat the lattice as [error-correcting code](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=XbL64sz8dQI): spread the pattern widely enough that syndrome measurement could find and mend the damage without ever reading a single protected life inside it. Nobody would need to look at what any citizen actually was. Only at whether the shape around them still held.

And the apparatus would be switched off first. If the scanners had been deforming the guidance, the most useful move before any clever repair was to stop making it worse.

"That's not physics," Fainrose objected. "That's just turning it off."\
"It is the least fashionable intervention available," Lolly countered. "De Broccoli didn't give me a new machine. He gave me permission to stop running the old one."

Gideon Wigglesworth, who had been sitting by the window with a coffee gone cold, said, "You'll still need to know where the off switch actually is."

Fainrose looked round. "I beg your pardon?"

"The scanners. The certification passes. The entire industrial machinery of asking, you said." Gideon set his cup down with care. "Nobody built one switch. They built forty, over fifteen years, in whatever cupboard was nearest. Nobody ever plans a shutdown; they only ever plan a launch. If you want it off, you don't need a theory. You need someone who has spent three years reading requisition forms and already knows which cupboard the relay is in."

"And you know," replied Lolly.\
"I know which nineteen cupboards it might be in," Gideon said. "I can find out the other one by Thursday." He stood to go and refill his coffee, misjudged the doorway by the same half-inch he had been misjudging it for as long as Lolly had known him, and did not, on this occasion, spill any of it, which she chose to take as a good omen.

Nobody asked what the plan became if it turned out to be cupboard twenty. This close to a deadline, it seemed unwise to find out by asking.

Beatrix wrote this down in the flattened, unpoetic language a court could not misread, and delivered it at four o'clock precisely. The Board, which had wanted a plan mainly in order to reject one, discovered it could not credibly call this absent, and was obliged, for the first time in the Initiative's history, to fund something rather than merely surviving a review of it.

Four interpretations, one afternoon, one deadline of four o'clock. It was the most ecumenical document the Ministry had ever been handed, and it contained not a single prayer. Beatrix, reading it, allowed herself the administrative equivalent of hope, which is a margin.

The deadline was no longer an administrative fiction. At 4C, the anchor had begun to lose mass: not much yet, a shaving of a shaving, but the curve runs one way and has never once waited for a signature. Every hour they spent arguing about interpretation was an hour of information sliding past the point where anyone, ever, could get it back.

Gideon looked at the clock. "We should probably stop debating whether we're ready."\
Beatrix glanced at him. "Why?"\
"Because the universe doesn't appear to have asked whether we are."

It was that same afternoon, with the plan on the Board's desk and not a scanner yet switched off, that Mrs Elspeth Chain came to the annex and asked to see Fainrose alone, which she had not done before.

While she waited, Lolly stood at the tall window at the end of the corridor, which nobody had cleaned since the Initiative began and which nonetheless gave, unexpectedly, the best view in the building. Somewhere among the wet slate roofs and scaffolding sat one of the private garden squares that turned up all over that part of London: a rectangle of real grass and real trees, locked behind black railings, key-holders only, visible to absolutely anyone and entered by almost no one. She had never worked out who decided which eleven households got a key and which got a view of other people's key. It was beginning to seem like the only question the Ministry had ever actually been answering.

A barrier, she thought, was only ever a classical promise. Quantum mechanically nothing was ever quite locked --- a particle without the energy to clear a wall could still, occasionally, arrive on the other side of it anyway, for no reason a key would recognise. She wondered, not for the first time, whether the eleven households had ever noticed how much of the grass on their side had simply leaked through from the pavement.

Lolly was not present for the conversation. She learned what mattered from Fainrose afterward, on the crate by the timing rack, with two mugs of the annex tea, which nobody had improved.

"She asked me for a number," Fainrose recounted. "How much of her sister we would get back. A percentage. She wanted it before the injunction ran out, not after, because she said she had spent her whole life being told good news arrived on time and had never once seen it happen and did not intend to be surprised twice."

"And you gave her one."

"I told her the truth, which is that I did not know, and that anybody who told her a number this week was lying to her for the same reason Voller lied to himself about the vault: because a number is comforting and a distribution is not, and she deserved the distribution."

Fainrose turned the mug in her hands. "She said that was the first honest answer anybody in this building had given her, and that she would come back when there was one worth hearing. Then she left without shouting, which frightened me considerably more than if she had."

"She asked me one more thing, on the doorstep," Fainrose added. "Not the number. She asked what she should do about the second duvet in the meantime --- air it weekly, or leave it folded. I told her I had no idea whether that would matter to a wave function." Something crossed her face that was not quite a smile. "She said, well, it will matter to the bed. And went home to air it."

Lolly thought about the photograph on the mantelpiece, restored by a man pulling a cable out of a skirting board without permission, and about the two duvets, and about a woman standing in a doorway in her own house asking a government to explain itself, and then going home to do the one ordinary thing that was still entirely hers to do.

"What do we actually owe her," she asked. "Not the district. Her."

Fainrose did not answer immediately, which she had learned to recognise as the shape of an answer arriving.

"I used to think the job was restoration," she admitted at last. "Put it back. That's what I trained for. But de Broccoli asked whether a fully restored district would be a country, or a forgery of the country that would have existed if nobody had touched it. I did not have an answer. I am unused to that, and I dislike it less than I expected."

She did not look at Lolly when she said it, which was, for Fainrose, its own kind of confession.

"What's the alternative to restoration."

"Not a cartoon. Not a copy. Not a photograph of a conversation." Fainrose looked at her. "A district that remembers, imperfectly, that a Ministry once stood in its wave with a clipboard. Mrs Chain wants her sister recoverable, not undamaged (my words, from week one, which she kept). None of the rest of us did."

Lolly opened the green notebook and found the page from the night of the cold vault, Fainrose's words, jotted beneath the note about the code, almost as an afterthought: She will be recoverable. That is not the same as unharmed.

"Then that's the plan," she considered slowly. "Not full-state reconstruction. Not the vault's dream of exactness. A negotiated settlement between what was and what can honestly be recovered: published, itemised, with the gaps named rather than papered over. Von Wittenberg's list, when it comes. Whatever fraction it turns out to be."

"A country that admits its own scar tissue," reflected Fainrose, "and not one that pretends to have been born without a wound."\
"Will the Board accept that."

"The Board," said Fainrose, "will accept whatever a court orders it to accept, and courts, it turns out, have a great deal of respect for an itemised list of gaps and no respect whatever for a certificate of perfection. Beatrix explained this to me on the second day and I did not believe her and I believe her now."

They sat for a while without talking, which by then had become one of the more comfortable things either of them did.

"I keep thinking about the moon," offered Lolly eventually.\
"Outer Fenwick."

"It didn't need forty-one people to stay a moon. It needed the air left alone to keep noticing it. That is what we're trying to give Mrs Chain back: not a certificate. Permission for an ordinary life to stay itself without a form standing over it."

"That is either the plainest sentence you have considered since breakfast," reflected Fainrose, "or the most sentimental, and I find I am too tired to tell which, which may itself be the first reliable instrument reading we have had all afternoon."

She wrote it down anyway.

Beatrix looked in from the corridor. "It's on their desk," she announced. "We have a plan. They have a Board. I know which of those I would rather take into a courtroom."

Somewhere under the Ministry's floor, four hundred and eleven states were still falling towards a horizon that had never heard of injunctions. The clock Beatrix had spent the whole day arguing about was not, in the end, the one that mattered most.

Rule: The honest plan repairs what survives and names what does not; it does not require picking an interpretation first.

Lesson for this chapter: Recovery Without Deciding

# **Chapter 16: The Decision**

The written order was sealed on the Tuesday, once the plan the Board had demanded on the Monday (the price of losing the hearing) reached the judge's desk. He understood none of the physics and all of the paperwork, which turned out to be sufficient. Beatrix Sloan had not argued that reality had been damaged. She had learned, by then, that courts do not adjudicate ontology. She had argued instead that the Ministry had certified nine thousand separate local determinations while holding, in its own filing system, a [measurement](#glMeasurement) establishing that they were one act; and that a body which certifies nine thousand times what it knows to be one thing has not created an error of physics but a false instrument in a live system. Which is a thing the law has known how to dislike since the fourteenth century, as the Duc de Broccoli observed with some satisfaction from the public gallery.

He did ask, once, from the bench, whether nine thousand certifications might mean nine thousand separate liabilities. Nobody in the room answered quickly enough to make it look accidental.

They got eleven weeks. It was not enough, and nobody had prepared a form in which enough was an available option. Von Wittenberg's people listened to the horizon for nine of them, from a rented industrial unit outside Sub-District 6, with equipment that cost more than the district's entire housing stock and had, for that reason, a more generous maintenance schedule, and that Fainrose described, in her only published remark on the matter, as "an ear held against a burning wall."

By the second week of November, with the rain settling into the flat, permanent grey that London kept in reserve for exactly this kind of year, the operation had stopped resembling physics and started resembling a hospital corridor: mostly waiting, occasionally interrupted by everyone in the room forgetting, briefly and in unison, how to breathe.

Twice in nine weeks the readings spiked hard enough that every technician in the annex stopped moving at once, certain this was the moment the horizon ran out of patience. Twice, for reasons nobody wrote down, it wasn't.

Von Wittenberg's team worked in shifts around a horizon that would not hold still. Numbers came back out of the radiation in fragments, the way a memory comes back to someone recovering from a fever: a name, a colour, a smell of toast, and then nothing for six hours. Fainrose stopped sleeping in any sense that would have satisfied an inquiry. Beatrix filed three emergency applications and lost none of them, which she treated, privately, as evidence that the universe had finally noticed her paperwork.

It was Jago Flint who noticed the other thing.

He had been carrying courier bags in and out of the annex for eleven weeks without once being asked to sit down, which meant he had spent eleven weeks looking at the coupling lattice the way nobody paid to understand it ever did: not as a diagram of what it was for, but as a knot. He wondered, the way anyone does with a knot, what would happen if you simply let it go.

"You could cut it," he offered, on the night of the thirteenth, to a room that had not asked him.

Fainrose didn't look up from the trace. "It's live."\
"So's the bit you're listening to," Jago noted. "You're not proposing to stop that." He shrugged, the particular shrug of a man who delivers difficult envelopes for a living and has made his peace with difficult envelopes. "I'm saying: you've got what you're going to get. The coupling's not bringing anything more back. It's just keeping the door open for whatever's still in there to come out the wrong way."

Nobody objected, which in a Ministry annex at one in the morning counted as unanimous consent.

Gideon Wigglesworth, who had spent the last two hours failing to locate a plug socket that was, by his own later admission, directly behind him, straightened up with abrupt clarity, having just understood something he wasn't trying to understand. "It's not a valve," he declared. "Everyone's been treating it like a valve. It's a---"

He stopped, visibly hunting for the word, found it, looked faintly betrayed by how obvious it now seemed. "It's a bridge. You don't turn a bridge off. You take it down."

Beatrix regarded him for a long moment. "That is either the most useful thing anyone has mentioned in this room in eleven weeks," she considered, "or you have been standing near a filing cabinet radiating something we should be measuring."\
"Possibly both," said Gideon, and walked, with some dignity, directly into the filing cabinet.

It was, Lolly thought, the most accurate model of his whole method she had ever watched performed live: solve it perfectly in the space where the answer actually lives, and only then discover, physically, exactly where the furniture had been standing the entire time.

Beatrix stated the rest of it plainly, because somebody had to, and it was, in the end, the sort of thing she was for. "We will not get a better number by waiting. Every hour we leave it live is an hour it charges us for the privilege of listening. Cut now, and we lock in whatever we've got. Wait, and we get less, and slower, and with more paperwork about it."

"What if it's less either way?" Lolly asked. She had not meant to say it aloud; it arrived the way the true question always did, several seconds behind the polite one.

Fainrose looked at her properly for the first time in an hour. "Then it's less." No softening, which was its own kind of honesty. "Nobody gets to know first, Lolly: that's not a service the universe provides, however many forms you file for it. I know what waiting costs. I don't know what cutting buys. I'm doing it anyway. Not doing it has a cost too, and at least this one I get to choose."

They cut the coupling at 06:04 on the fourteenth of November. It was not dramatic to watch. That was the problem. A technician in a borrowed coat opened a junction box that had not been opened since the annex was built --- according to a maintenance log that was, on this point, wrong. She located the correct relay by the light of a torch held in her teeth. She removed it the way one removes a fuse. The junction box itself had been found three days earlier by Gideon Wigglesworth, who had gone through nineteen requisition ledgers by hand and got it right on the first try, a fact he mentioned only once and only when nobody appeared to be listening.

Whether it was wrong about anything else was a question nobody had time to ask twice.

Lolly's tea had gone cold an hour ago. She did not put it down. She watched the woman's hand and the small dark mouth of the empty fuse slot and felt, with humiliating clarity, that if she breathed too loudly she might tip four hundred and eleven pending states: including Margaret Chain, including Dorothy Kell's brother, including every name that had been reduced to a certificate.

Nobody forced a joke. For eleven seconds nothing happened, which was the longest eleven seconds of her professional life: one entire district balanced on a torch battery and a steady hand.

Even Beatrix Sloan, who had spent eleven weeks being audibly certain about everything, said nothing at all for the whole eleven seconds, and did not reach for her pen until it was over --- which Lolly understood to be the nearest thing to fear that Beatrix Sloan permitted herself in public.

Then the coherence monitor Fainrose had left running since Sub-District 6, flat at 1.2 for eleven weeks, with the patience of something that had given up being interesting, climbed to 2.79 in slightly under forty minutes, and stopped.

Fainrose read it twice. "That's disconnection," she reported, in the voice of someone reporting a result she had stopped allowing herself to expect. "Clean disconnection. The number climbs because the houses can finally hold together with one another again --- they'd been spending all of it on the anchor, and the anchor's let go. It's not listening to us any more. It's just a black hole. An ordinary, boring, evaporating black hole, doing the only thing black holes are supposed to do, which is leave."

Jago, without quite deciding to, checked the topmost envelope in his bag --- the one that had spent eleven weeks unable to settle on a single street. The address had stopped assembling itself. It held still, in one place, like something that had finally been allowed to finish deciding. He did not mention it to anyone. Some things a courier learns to file under good news and leave alone.

"So we have them all back," Lolly blurted, before she could stop herself.

"No." Fainrose didn't soften that either. "We have what didn't cost anything to keep this long. The horizon's been eating information for eleven weeks, and every bit it ate, it paid for in heat. That's not poetry --- that's [Landauer's Principle](#glLandauers). [Erase a bit, you owe the universe warmth](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=uc9P5yb3Xtc), no exceptions, no appeals process. Some of it didn't come back scrambled --- it came back spent, and there's no ledger entry for spent. Wherever the books balance, it isn't in a column we can read."

Nobody did the arithmetic aloud. Someone was clearly doing it silently, because the quality of the silence in the annex changed.

Gideon, still extracting himself from the filing cabinet, said, "So it isn't that we can't find them. It's the entropy. Boltzmann's constant --- S equals k log omega --- and really, what is thermalisation but particles getting Boltz'd around until everyone agrees on a distribution? Hence: Boltzmann distribution---"\
"Yes," confirmed Fainrose, before he could finish being helpful.

Nobody cheered. Lolly noted, and later wrote in the green notebook, that this seemed correct: there is no ceremony appropriate to successfully stopping a government department from continuing to be haunted.

Of the four hundred and eleven P-9 states, they recovered three hundred and eight. It was not a triumph, and Fainrose refused, in every interview afterwards, to let it be described as one.

They were pending human states, unscrambled from the radiation and returned to the districts they had come out of: a child's route to school, a marriage in the middle of being decided, an apprenticeship, two reconciliations, a great many small unfinished intentions of no civic significance whatever.

One recovered intention turned out to be nothing more than a decision, made eleven weeks earlier and never acted on, to repaint a kitchen. Nobody knew which form covered that, procedurally, so they filed it under recovered and left it there.

One hundred and three were not recovered, and their names were published, because Beatrix insisted, and because Miss Dorothy Kell, whose brother's apprenticeship was one of the hundred and three, and who had written to the inquiry every week since the freeze, said that a list of the unrecovered was the only memorial such things could have.

Lolly read the list twice, standing in the annex corridor with cold tea in her hand, and found that her cleverness had nothing useful to say. She wrote Margaret Chain's name in the green notebook under RECOVERED, and then, underneath, in smaller letters that were not for any inquiry: one hundred and three people the Ministry will never get to apologise to properly. It was the first thing she had written all week that was not, in some sense, a form.

Rule: You can close the coupling; you cannot unspend the heat already paid.

Lesson for this chapter: [Thermodynamics](#lesson-16-thermodynamics)

# **Chapter 17: A Very Good Obituary**

The districts resolved over the following month, badly and thinly and in the wrong order. On every screen it looked like [collapse](#glCollapse) --- a screen can only report one outcome at a time, and the public had stopped telling the reporting from the event. That was why Crispin Vale, who had testified for eleven days against the directive he had signed, was blamed for the disorder the recovery made, not credited for making the recovery lawful.

Whether the Ministry meant, eventually, to correct the record, or simply intended to let a useful silence do the work an exoneration would have done more loudly, nobody in a position to answer was answering.

Fainrose had considered that would happen; knowing it in advance never lowered the cost. She took no visible pleasure in being right.

The Fenwick mast was found in the weeks after the coupling was cut: a decorative weathercock on the Memorial Hall with considerably more electronics under the tail than weathercocks generally require, and a small plate reading

> PROPERTY OF THE SIMPLIFICATION INITIATIVE

Nobody standing there was in a hurry to touch it. Whatever else the Initiative had failed at, it had not failed at making people nervous about screwdrivers.

Jago opened it with a screwdriver he was not supposed to be carrying. "Well," he said, "that explains the improved weather forecasts."\
Mrs Prosser arrived with tea, confirmed what fourteen months of moon rota had already taught her, and watched the Ministry switch the mast off eleven days later, apologise without once using the word sorry, and send her a revised quorum of thirty-nine --- a number nobody had voted on, which was rather the point. "It is encouraging," she remarked, "to see government respond to evidence."

Detective Inspector Albert Priddy convened the interested parties in the Memorial Hall on the last Thursday of the month, arranged nine folding chairs into a semicircle facing one battered armchair he did not sit in, and opened a notebook whose corners were, Lolly noticed and declined to mention, exactly as soft with handling as her own.

The chairs, being village-hall folding chairs, produced between them a small percussion of scrapes and clangs as nine people tried to sit down simultaneously without looking like they were taking sides.

"I have asked you all here," he announced, with the visible pleasure of a man who had waited some time to say it, "because I believe I have, at last, established what happened to Unit 7B."

Jago brightened. Mrs Prosser produced a thermos. Beatrix urged, "Go on, then," in the tone of someone settling in for a play she suspected would not end well.

The hall smelled, as village halls always do, of damp coir matting, the tea urn's tannic scorch, and several decades of jumble sales no open window had ever quite cleared, with a paraffin tang underneath it from a heater someone had lit in direct defiance of the fire notice pinned by the door.

Priddy consulted his notes. Over six weeks he had taken forty-one statements regarding the scorch marks on the Equivalent Residence Unit, the address plates on Chain Terrace that periodically disagreed with the postman, and a certain quantity of missing time nobody could account for and everybody had a theory about. He had cross-referenced whereabouts. He had, he said, established a picture.

"The damage to Unit 7B," he announced, "was caused, between the hours of nine and half past, by Mr Cyril Pargeter of Number Nine, acting alone, out of a grievance regarding the noise." He said noise the way other men said arson.

There was a silence of the sort Priddy had learned, over a long career, to enjoy.

"He has no alibi," he continued. "Three witnesses place him passing Unit 7B at the relevant hour."\
"Four, if you ask them differently," Jago said.\
Priddy's pencil paused. "I beg your pardon?"

"Ask Mrs Ainsworth and Mr Deeks together, and they'll tell you Pargeter went by at nine and stayed put till half past. Ask Mrs Ainsworth and the postman, and Pargeter was home the whole time and never left his garden. Ask the postman and Mr Deeks on their own, without Mrs Ainsworth in the room, and you get a third answer, and none of the three pairs agree with any other pair, though each pair is perfectly certain of itself." Jago spread his hands, not unkindly. "I did try telling you this on Tuesday."

Priddy had, in fact, a note to this effect from Tuesday, heavily underlined, with the word IMPOSSIBLE written beside it and then crossed out and replaced with the word LATER. "Every alibi has a hole in it if you look closely enough," he stated, with rather less certainty than the sentence usually carried. "I shall simply need to re-establish---"

"You could re-establish it forty times, Inspector," observed Beatrix, gently, in the tone one uses on a man about to buy a house with a serious structural problem, "and get forty different Pargeters. It isn't that he's a bad witness. It's that the street doesn't hold still long enough to have a whereabouts."

Fainrose, who had come only to observe and regretted it slightly less than she had expected to, examined the report over Priddy's shoulder. "These marks are consistent with Unit 7B's own settling cycle," she noted. "It sheds structure it can no longer support. Nobody damaged it. It simply declined to be one thing for the whole evening."\
"As did Mr Pargeter, evidently," offered Mrs Prosser, not entirely helpfully.

Priddy closed his notebook slowly, the way a man closes a door on a room he fully intends to come back to. "Then there is no case," he stated, "which I shall write up as thoroughly as if there were." He gathered his coat. At the door he paused, glanced at Lolly's own notebook open on her knee, and permitted himself something almost like a colleague's nod. "Keep at yours," he added. "One of us is bound to get to the bottom of something, eventually." He did not sound as though he believed it, and did not, Lolly thought, sound entirely wrong either.

"Your lot get a Christmas party out of it, at least," Lolly noted.\
"We get a memo about biscuit allowances," Priddy replied, "and even that went to committee."

He was aware, even as he said it, exactly how the sentence would read to his superintendent, who took an unsolved file rather more personally than he took an innocent man, and had said as much, twice, in writing. Priddy wrote it anyway, in his own hand, and did not soften a word of it.

Lolly delivered Mrs Elspeth Chain's certificate herself, on a Tuesday in March, because she had learned by then that some documents should not simply be posted.

Twenty-Two Coldharrow Rise, where Mrs Chain had been moved when the old house was condemned, smelled of instant coffee and furniture polish, applied in that order and recently. Mrs Chain read the certificate at her own kitchen table, twice, without comment, while Lolly sat opposite and did the thing she had got, over the winter, genuinely good at: nothing.
